ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u77	Mr President, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, it is a pleasure - I would like to stress - to speak to an empty Chamber, empty as are the classrooms of the bicameral committees, many times convened and also deserted by my fellow senators and MPs of the PD and the PdL who say that they have so much to care about the problems of Italian citizens and that they do not come to the convocations because they do not reach the agreement - I repeat: they do not reach the agreement - on who should hold the much desired office. With regard to the bill we are debating today, I say that on a few historic occasions we can change those fundamental documents that regulate life in our society and regulate what our rights are. This is not the time. The changes presented by the so-called Committee of Wise Men call for a radical change in our form of government, a change that, being constitutional, will affect future generations; therefore those who live this historical moment should realize what are the characteristics that make decisive historical moments suitable to make great works of change for the whole nation. The change that the majority are preparing to make is certainly not the result of a popular decision but of a will for unilateral change of political forces. It is true that a change is needed, but that is not the change that citizens are asking for. Constitutional rules, senators, are the guiding principles of every legal practitioner, whether he be a magistrate, a lawyer or a job consultant. They are words, even taken individually, that guide us towards the interpretation of our rules, they are criteria and directives in the application of everyday legislation. Efforts to radically change a part of the card require a very broad consensus that the current majority does not possess, either as regards numbers or as regards stability. The broad understandings were immediately put to the test and it is clear to all that there are no lasting agreements to work according to the growth of this country, but only temporary partnerships destined to dissolve at the first warning of elections. In view of this confusing, uncertain and devoid of guarantees and stability, how is it possible to put hand to the fundamental mechanisms of our society, without the security that comes from a broad and shared popular consensus? Our present Charter is largely unenforced in all those principles that were so clear when our Republic was born. On the application of these fundamental parts the current political forces stage a theater - yours, not ours - [[Applause from the M5S Group]] with the connivance of journalists, for twenty years now, making us believe that the goal is now near, ever closer but derogating and extending, I repeat, derogating and extending situations in which the abuse of law reigns, abuse that is then legitimized as time passes. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] These premises do not push for a rapid and sudden change, they do not give certainties about the magnitude of these changes. To derogate even once from the timetable laid down in Article 138 means to ignore all the struggles against the fog of the powerful to unilaterally regulate the conditions of their subjects, who at this point are only subjects. The long times dictated by Article 138 are the fruit of wars, deaths and sacrifices, sacrifices aimed at building a text that was solid and resisted the pitfalls of authoritarian power. These struggles were carried out so that the rights established after the French Revolution were absolute rights! And not mere faculties recognized at will by the sovereigns to whom all was permitted. From a purely legal point of view, it is clear that the rigidity of our fundamental Charter is defended precisely by the rule to which the majority are preparing to derogate. The desire to put hand in the first place to this rule hides a purely political and shamelessly biased design. This is not just any part, it is the old policy that wants to get rid of the obstacles to the expression of pure power, claiming emergencies to legislate on any subject in any way. [[Applause from Senator Bottici]] Here too, we want to legislate quickly and without effective control by the people to whom the amended text would apply (because it is the citizens we have to account for). [[Applause from the M5S Group]] The draft law under consideration does not only change the timing of the transition between one House and the other, but also changes the process of constitutional revision itself. It is the legitimacy of the elected, who are asked to vote silently on this amendment, which is a matter of concern. These statements also start from a fundamental consideration: with the current electoral law, those residing in the legislative classrooms are not elected, but appointed by the parties to which they belong. With an electoral legitimacy of this kind, can we create a constituent Assembly as it was in 1948? The distance is abysmal and not only in temporal terms. The result can only be a delegitimized product in its final substance because it is not an expression of the will of the people. A Constitution generated in this incomplete and forced way, imposed and overturned in a fateful way does not reflect what is the soul of this nation. The 1948 Charter was the expression of the liberal currents that forged the Italian Republic. It is a text whose provisions have not been settled, pending a law applying them. Therefore, instead of reforming them, apply them; instead of overturning them, implement them! Of course, those men came out of a war and from twenty years of dictatorship and were afraid: fear that the war would return, fear that the dictatorship had a regurgitation, and for this they adopted a system that sometimes seems complicated, machinous. Fear is not a healthy feeling, noble, of those who intend to preserve their fellow citizens from the dangers of a dictatorship or, God forbid, a war. ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u83	Mr President, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to speak briefly on a very important subject, and I am pleased that the Minister is also present and can take into account some comments concerning the Commission's report and the report that the Minister wanted to make in the Senate yesterday on a particular point, namely the possible reform of Title V. As we rightly say, it is a very complex reform from a technical and political point of view, because it depends on the possible improvements that we can expect, which are desirable both in terms of the efficiency of the whole system and of the fairness with which we respond to the needs of the citizens. There is one point that is very close to my heart and that, unfortunately, is not contained in the pages that concern the reform of Title V, which I hope is of common interest and on which I want to focus attention. When decentralized, the fundamental principle is to ensure that decentralisation can be accompanied by the guarantee of the rights of all citizens throughout the national territory under conditions of uniformity, regardless of the technical and political quality of the various public administrations. For this reason, the reform of Title V of 2001 had introduced very important concepts. The first is that of the essential levels of benefits, which remain the exclusive competence of the State, and the second, according to Article 120 of the Constitution, had provided that if the subnational levels of government did not guarantee the essential levels of benefits relating to the civil and social rights of citizens and which must be guaranteed in conditions of uniformity, the State could replace the decentralised subnational levels of government. In particular, Article 120 provided for this to be one of the cases in which the powers of the central level could be exercised. It is an issue of great importance, on which too little work has been done during this decade, and it is extremely important, because otherwise we end up decentralising only the objective of empowering decentralised bodies to respect public finance constraints, and not also to respect the rights of civil and social citizens when they are recognized and, therefore, to be guaranteed throughout the national territory. In many areas, particularly health, but also social and, in future, in other areas too, if we do not ensure monitoring and intervention if the administrations, in order to comply with budgetary constraints, do not respect the essential levels or simply worry about spending no more on the roof they are given, but not on guaranteeing services, we make two mistakes in one. Firstly, we do not push them to be more efficient in using resources when possible and, secondly, we do not push them to be able to guarantee uniformity also in those Regions, where the situation from the organisational point of view of the functioning of the public administration is more lacking than in other Regions. The fact that the wise men's report speaks of this safeguard clause, but no longer cites respect for the essential levels of benefits relating to civil and social rights, by mentioning only programmes of national interest, is very worrying. It is a debate, which had already taken place at the beginning of the previous decade, about the role that the existence must play first and, then, the monitoring of the essential levels of performance. My great concern is that on this issue we will return to uncertain ideas and fiscal federalism serves only to guarantee - if it succeeds - compliance with the parameters of public finance, and not a greater uniformity of citizens' rights on the ground. [[Applause from the PD Group.]] (at 13.44 p.m.)

Before I deal with the substance of this draft law, I would like to go upstream and recall today's political conditions in which this draft law is being discussed. In this House there is a government supported by a majority, so to speak, anomalous. It is a majority that does not reflect what was the electoral offer during the campaign for the past parliamentary elections this winter. It is a majority which, if the voters of the parties that make up this majority had known it, probably these same parties would not have had the consent they had. We are, therefore, in an abnormal situation. Moreover, this majority was born, started and continues to be a mess, because it oscillates continuously on different positions (for example, the case of VAT or the IMU are emblematic) and is constantly subject to pressure from the right and left, precisely because it is extremely heterogeneous. Dulcis in the end, it is also the subject of the pressures of Silvio Berlusconi, who tried, with alternating fortunes, to tie his judicial affairs with the political path of this majority. All this has generated an objectively very unstable political situation, so much so that within a few months, already twice, this majority has been at risk and has come to this House to ask for confidence. The first time, a prominent person of this majority, the Vice-President of the Council, was the subject of a motion of distrust, which obviously, if it had passed, would have had very serious repercussions; lately, even a short while ago, there was even a request for confidence from the Government in its entirety. We are, therefore, faced with a very unstable political situation. The same President Letta, at the beginning, in his programmatic speech, has given himself a time horizon that is not that of the entire legislature. Already he himself, therefore, realized that the majority, of which he is the leader at the moment, still has a limited time horizon and suffers from a continuous political instability. I now ask myself: are there, therefore, at this moment, the political conditions for addressing a sensitive issue such as constitutional reforms? On the other hand, is there not the risk that the instability which this majority suffers will be transferred equal in the process of constitutional reform, which should, in my opinion, take place under other conditions, those in which there is a stable majority, which can of course also respect the views of the minority, and not instead of a majority that already starts on the wrong foot, already divided within it, let alone if we can respect and take into account the instances of the minority? Would it not therefore be appropriate to postpone these reforms to a new legislature, which is born with a new electoral law, so as to overcome the problems, known to all, of the electoral law that gave rise to this majority? I refer, in particular, to the fact that citizens can directly elect their parliamentarians, rather than they are appointed by the parties. Is it not appropriate, then, to pause for a moment to reflect on this? We are talking here, ladies and gentlemen, Minister, about a very important reform, not just any reform: a reform that affects a large part of the Constitution (almost half of it is mentioned). So this process should take place when the political conditions are more appropriate for a constitutional reform process to be carried out, to which we are not a priori opposed, which is widely shared not only by the majority, but also by oppositions. Well, these conditions are not there today. Today there is a majority that is hostage to a political character, who does not have a long-term strategic vision of the necessary measures for this country, and I am referring to measures that concern the world of work or the world of education. There is no long-term strategy in this government, let alone a vision of the constitutional structure of our country. Colleagues, with these constitutional reforms we are planning tomorrow's Italy, we are planning the future, but not the future in the short term, the future of the coming months: we are planning a new country, a vision of Italy in the long term, what will be in five or ten years, an Italy in which our children will live; therefore this process must take place with the utmost attention, when the political conditions are favourable to do so, and these conditions are not there today. It is also for this reason that we oppose this bill. And then, apart from these general considerations, there are those of a particular nature, which enter into the substance and the way in which this majority wants to implement this reform. This committee of forty parliamentarians is born and the process that the Constitution itself envisaged for the change of itself is overturned. In particular, there is a reduction in the terms of the parliamentary passages of the constitutional reform bill. Now, let us ask ourselves: why this change to Article 138 at this time? It is precisely for the reasons I have just said, which concern the political conditions under which these reforms are to be carried out. For these very reasons of instability, however, in our view, there is no need for the reduction of these time limits; on the contrary, we should take advantage of all the guarantees that Article 138 gives us at this time, which are also those of the thoughtfulness of the interventions. These three months separating the two parliamentary passages are, in fact, a guarantee of thought, in such a way that the country, not only us in this House, but the whole country, has the time to assess on the merits what are the changes of our Constitution. Precisely because they could come from a more anomalous majority, like the one there is right now in this country. That is precisely why there is this need. So, now and more than ever we would need fully the guarantees that the constituent Fathers themselves have included in the Constitution to protect hasty changes, hasty changes, changes that perhaps aim more at short-term electoral reasoning than at a vision of a new country, which is the one that should really lead at this time and that should be the drive to these changes. And then there's the country and there's the citizens. There is the mobilization of citizens. Surely you have not been deaf to the debates that this constitutional bill has generated in the country. There are constitutionalists who have intervened and there has been a collection of signatures from an important daily newspaper in our country. Well, the voice of these citizens is a voice that recommends prudence, it is a voice that recommends attention, it is a voice that once again asks not to upset the Constitution itself in order to achieve its change. It is a voice that calls for change (attention, because the need for change is shared by a large part of the citizens and a large part of the political forces). So, yes to change, but no to the hasty and abnormal way in which you want to make these changes. A no to twisting the way the constituent Fathers wanted the Constitution to change. Well, I hope that colleagues in this House, when they vote on this constitutional bill, will take full account of all these considerations, which are yes political, but also considerations that come from all the citizens. And I hope that we can come to a halt to this constitutional decree and that instead we can channel the necessary and due changes to the Constitution according to the tracks already foreseen for these changes. ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u94	Madam President, Minister, representatives of the Government, thank you for your attention. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, all of us, neighbours and distant citizens, today I would also like to thank the stenographer's party, which seems to be a majority among us. Madam President, allow me to begin my speech with a preamble, a very important point: never underestimate the man on the road. I will argue this expression from afar. Our Constitution was written after the Second World War, after fascism and after Nazism, after one of the darkest and saddest moments in human history. In the constituent Assembly, a Sub-Committee of 75 Italians, between 1946 and 1948, wrote some of the most touching, right and necessary words ever written before. In 139 articles of the Constitution we find all the antidotes to the barbarity that preceded them: principles and human rights, equality, freedom of thought and expression, freedom of association and much more, but also a wise mixing and redistribution of power to ensure that no man could rule alone anymore. Today the Committee of the 40 Wise Men has had the task, and the permission, of putting in hand 66 articles of the 139 of the Charter, which will be modified in a time of about one and a half years. So, we're actually talking about an assembly, at least semicostituente. In the first discussion here in the Senate we of the 5 Star Movement strongly opposed each other. We are so many citizens, it is true, but perhaps still not enough to stop these actions. We believe that such a complex and extensive reform should start from a higher level of awareness, both in the recent past and in the present. So what are the assumptions and awareness behind this reform? This committee of essays, made up of Chinese boxes of essays, what do you know? And on what grounds? Let's see them. They do not know, for example, that we come from a period of great disinformation; they do not believe that their political class has caused the crisis and the discontent and anger that wanders around the country; they do not admit that, being the cause of all this, they cannot themselves be able to resolve the issues; they do not know that the whole country has now perceived that this government, political, has only one political opposition: the 5 Star Movement, caste against people, privilege against sacrifice; they do not want to take away privileges from those who have never made sacrifices. ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u96	Madam President, the first Commission that indicated a series of constitutional reforms was the Bozzi Commission in 1984; thirty years later, several attempts later, it is decided that, in order to change the Constitution, the rule itself must first be changed. The crisis of politics, the crisis of parties, of those who have to compete with the democratic method to determine national politics - as our Constitution says - has not allowed in recent years to face those nodes that not even yet another Commission of Wise Men, set up to suggest solutions, succeeds in the end to face. One hundred and sixty pages of report to hear us say that we must regenerate the parties, that governments must be faster and more efficient, that we must revive democratic participation, ensure efficiency and stability in the political system and strengthen public ethics. The proposal that is presented is to change the procedure by which the Constitution is amended. It has been proposed to make a Charter which, if it had been applied in its entirety, would in all likelihood have prevented or at least not exploded the problems that we want to solve today. This is how we pursue an old vice of our Republic: the emergency, a much more useful method to do what we want without political confrontation and without the responsibility to make choices of perspective. Today the only real emergency for all is the implementation of the Constitution, including Article 49. Piero Calamandrei, who has been quoted in this House today, said that in Italy it is not possible to achieve a complete democracy if those who are not truly democratic are also those who are responsible for determining national policy. If we want to try to change Italy, then, we make the parties transparent and democratic in their actions, as well as in the transparent methods of financing. The Minister has repeatedly made it clear that the only thing he really cares about is the stability of the form of government, the effectiveness of political action. As after bribes the crisis of representation of the parties deepened, the response of the Italian political class was only to flee from the problem in order to pursue shortcuts, blaming its own crisis on the electoral system: the overcoming of pure proportional representation, the abolition of the preference vote, bipolarism, until reaching a reform of the electoral law made to ensure a coalition to maximize the advantage in the regions where it was stronger, in case it had won the other; it is the case of the "porcellum." Well, although parliamentarians were chosen by the leaders of the parties, we witnessed passages from the coats of arms, we had weak and hybrid governments, and this was the consequence and cause of the growing weakness of the political forces, which were no longer able to represent social groups, were perceived as groups of power, of interest, far from the needs of the citizens. Is it the fault of the rules if there have not been majorities capable of effectively governing even in the presence of huge numbers? Are the scandals and ugliness of politics the fault of an old constitution? We believe that it is precisely the absence of politics that is responsible for this. The theme that today is strongly imposed on us all, is how to rebuild the relationship between citizens and institutions, between citizens and politics; how to reactivate that democratic participation that has allowed the country, in the past years, to make real reforms of civilization. The level of mistrust and resignation of the citizens is all in the dramatic fact of abstention in the last elections: 25% of Italian citizens do not have parliamentary representation, just as they are not represented, thanks to the "porcellum," all those who voted other political forces, but have not reached the barrier threshold. That has become a worthless vote! So how do we respond to this crisis of participation? By reducing the number of Members of Parliament, on which we would still be prepared to confront each other. This seems to be the cause of bad politics and waste. I believe that we must do a work of truth and clarity. The reduction in the number of Members of Parliament, in this way, means reducing democratic representation spaces. We are really talking about reducing the costs of Members without necessarily having to cut participation and democracy. Reduce elected assemblies means subtracting citizens from powers of direction, verification and control over governments. This idea of the reduction of democracy in the name of spending review you have already pursued with the reduction of municipal councils, where the number of elected has narrowed to allow those who govern to do so indisturbedly, and many, too many in the name of populism are those who have been convinced that in order to reduce waste it was necessary to cut democracy. Yet we must ask ourselves why, during the constituent phase, in a less populated Italy, 630 Members and 315 senators were chosen. Today we understand well the logic of this reform: transforming the parties into electoral committees, strengthening the tendency to personalize politics and the level of the executive with respect to Parliament, which is thus reduced in its powers and becomes a sort of assembly of shareholders with certain powers of control, minimal powers of direction with respect to the council of administration represented by the Government. It is not to put at the centre the democratization of forms of representation, providing for rules of extended democracy and transparency for parties, duties of information and transparency for representatives, all, from municipal to regional councillors, to parliamentarians, including Ministers; so much so, as the Minister has told us, we will give citizens a condolences with some initiative of direct democracy. Last Saturday thousands and thousands of citizens in Rome recalled that the Constitution must be implemented, that it is the main way of politics. It hasn't happened in years to see so many people in the streets defending democracy. We of Left Ecology and Freedom were there, because we are aware that a real battle of civilization and the defence of rights is going on. What is most disconcerting is how all this has been ignored, but just look at the low profile of the majority, the few speeches, the total absence in this House. I understand that it is hard for those who have to accept a bad proposal: it is easier not to listen to the debate, not to listen to the citizens; rather than to read the parliamentary reports! Yet a few months ago, in this very Chamber, there were authoritative voices from the Democratic Party in the debate to say that they would never and never vote for a change in the Constitution and the form of government, in the name of a defence of Article 138. And now? What's changed? Months ago we thought that semi-presidentialism was a request of the PdL placed on the table of broad understandings, today, in front of a deeply changed political scenario, we have the strong doubt - but very strong - that the truth can be another. In reading the text we remain very unarmed by the poverty of cultural proposal and the lack of imagination that emerges from these documents; I am thinking only of the issue of vote on a fixed date. In short, I believe that with this vote that you are preparing to express and with which you are undertaking this process of reform, you assume a great responsibility, which is certainly to cancel the parliamentary prerogatives, precisely those prerogatives that were introduced to protect democracy, and you do it in a truly shameful way, through a real dictatorship of the majority. [[Applause from the Mixed-SEL and M5S Groups.]]

ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u98	Madam President, Minister, members of the Government, citizens, I would like to remind you again of the text of Article 138 of the Constitution of the Italian Republic: "The laws of revision of the Constitution and other constitutional laws are adopted by each Chamber" - I repeat: by each Chamber - "with two successive resolutions at an interval of not less than three months, and are approved by an absolute majority of the members of each Chamber" - I repeat again: each Chamber - "in the second vote." The laws themselves are subject to a popular referendum when, within three months of their publication, one fifth of the members of a House or five hundred thousand voters or five regional councils apply. The law submitted to a referendum is not promulgated unless it is approved by a majority of valid votes. There is no referendum if the law was passed in the second vote by each of the Chambers - I repeat: each of the Chambers - is a two-thirds majority of its members. This is the text of Article 138. The draft law No 813-B, as it is written and presented to us, aims to set up a parliamentary committee for constitutional and electoral reforms, composed of 20 senators and 20 deputies, appointed by their respective presidents, and the Presidents of the parliamentary committees, to which the Presidency of the Committee itself is entrusted, are legally members. All in the family! And the dirty clothes of course wash in the family, wash them in the family! And yes, because it can be recognized conventionally and only that the father, Piddielle, married in civic marriage his mother, Piddimenoelle, marriage from which the saddles were born, all tied in autonomy: this is the real paradox and, in fact, they all lived unhappy and discontented. But let's stay on the piece, as they say today in jargon. This Committee, which should be set up, will have the task of examining or drafting draft constitutional revision laws, the systems of election of the two Chambers (the same Chambers which were mentioned in Article 138 of the Constitution) and the amendments to the Constitution. In addition, it is specified that the operating expenses of the Committee are charged, in equal parts, to the internal budget of the Senate of the Republic and the Chamber of Deputies. And other expenses down! At this point I quote part of the text of Mr Blundo's speech, which you, Minister, have not been able to listen to for health reasons, but it is appropriate to know the figures: We have unemployment at 12%, youth unemployment has reached 40%, 15 million Italians live in a condition of deprivation and economic hardship, we have a school dropout that has reached unprecedented percentages, a decrease in consumption and the purchasing power of wages that other European countries do not envy us at all. As many as 12,442 companies closed in 2012, to which we add the 4,218 that in the first quarter of this year delivered the books to the Court of Auditors - figures never seen, yet they appear - these are the priorities that this House has to face, but according to the reasoning of the Government and the majority the introduction of the direct election of the Head of State or the strengthening of the powers of the Prime Minister would allow the Italian citizens to face the day serenely. We of the 5 Star Movement are certain that, in doing so, the power conferred on the Chambers will be definitively exonerated, leaving to them only the possibility to vote on the changes made: beyond the damage, even the mockery! To date, the Chambers have legislative power and the Government is left with executive power. Today you want to turn everything upside down. But let's move on. There are many other parts that do not convince us: the compression of parliamentary work, the limits of amendment to Members of Parliament, the reduction of the prerogatives of individual Members and senators, the dangerous possibility of extending the revision to other articles of the Constitution (so much, done one, done all: this - who knows - could be one of the intentions), the lack of attention to the publicity of the Committee's work, the absolute lack of transparency of the Commission of so-called wise men. They are all elements of irrationality that put the text under consideration in a dark and conspiracy light. This appears to us. It is also indicative that in the first resolution the bill n. 813-B never exceeded the quorum of two thirds, neither the Senate nor the Chamber. The question at this point arises spontaneously. Why do we want to avoid this debate in this House? Why so much haste to change the basic rules of our Constitution? Why should power be concentrated in the hands of the Government and the Committee, by eliminating that legislative function which, since 1 January 1948, has been the main role of Parliament? Who or what do you want to facilitate? All this you have to give to the Italian citizens. You know that this reform is not the country's priority. You know well that at this moment we must give stability to those who do not have a job and to those who, unfortunately, are going to lose it: to the precarious, to pensioners, to the young people who want to build a future and a family, but who cannot do so. You know everything and know everything, especially you know how to keep the chair already conquered. Yet, a bill such as that on citizenship income - which we have been proposing for a long time, while you are a merchant - would be enough to give support to all those people who struggle day by day, to the limit of survival. It would be enough, it would be your ransom. I don't want to believe you're just capable of turning around. It is not possible that you want only this and that you want to carry out unnecessary and costly projects. I would like to believe that there is no TAV or TAP and no megagalactic construction, which devastates the territory and enriches only the multinationals and, perhaps, someone else's pockets. Once again, we ask you - it is the citizens who ask you and urge you to reflect constantly on this - to demonstrate that they are not light years away from people's everyday lives. But words remain empty, as this House is often empty, because you do not want to change this country. You insist on changing the rules that govern this country, and why? To mold them as you please. The message that we want to leave today is hope, the hope - vain - that one day a fateful enlightenment will come and it is possible to awaken from this bad dream. I want to conclude my speech with a sentence, which has a particular meaning for us in the 5 Star Movement: They will never give up. But does he want to? We don't either! ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u99	Senator Gotor is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u100	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to thank the Minister for the constitutional reforms for the comprehensive information he made yesterday in this House. I have had the opportunity to read the Commission's acts and the individual details and it seems to me that the Commission has been guided by a sense of shared responsibility and that it has done quality work. For a verse, he tried to identify a common language among constitutionalists of different orientations, with the aim of verifying the practical and technical feasibility of a series of reasonable solutions for the concrete Italian context, thus avoiding the tastes, often self-referential, of an abstract modeling. In another verse, the sense of responsibility has manifested itself in compliance with the time limits set before the beginning of the work. The Commission, as the Minister pointed out yesterday, was due to adopt a report by 15 October, but already on 17 September there was a provisional draft of the final version. It is an excellent signal, because the work of constitutional reform must be lucid, but accompanied by a feeling of urgency and hard work: we cannot pretend to have before us an indefinite time, because so we will only amplify the sense of inconclusivity of politics. As indicated by President Letta's schedule, eighteen months are the time necessary to dedicate oneself to the enterprise and to have begun in compliance with the set deadlines, it is not only a sign of seriousness of intent, but also a good viatico for the continuation of the works. Timely, effective and destined to last over time, these are the reforms that Italy awaits from us. Reading the report reveals two completely shared underlying points. The first recognizes the need for a change of Part II of the Constitution, starting from a guiding principle stated to the constituent Assembly of 1946 by Luigi Einaudi: It is not from the letter of a Constitution that we need to derive more fruitful elements: the letter was written in other times, when the needs were different; it is more interesting to see what use has been made of that system created many years ago. At the same time, we will never tire of repeating the indisputable value of Part I of the Charter dedicated to fundamental principles, which constitute the foundation of our common home. Not because Part II of the Charter should be isolated from I, but because I can only be implemented if II really works. The second point concerns the conviction, which Mr Quagliariello reiterated yesterday, that a democracy without parties is not even conceivable. It is precisely the strength of this assumption, however, that obliges us to recognize that the current parties are today in difficulty in fulfilling their constitutional functions: the representation of society and of what is tumultuous and elusive is agitated within it; the selection of the ruling class; the elaboration of long-term strategies. Rather, they seem crushed on a perennial electoral horizon, amplified by the communication system, which has raised the rate of militancy and internal and external division, in pursuit of a consensus without a project, which tends to increase in reality their progressive delegitimization. There are several good reasons for this exceptional reform effort, some quotas, others long-term. First of all, the current majority in government is justified precisely by the commitment made before Parliament in this area and it would be a serious mistake to waste this opportunity of necessity. Secondly, it is good to recall that the President of the Republic has conditioned his exceptional re-election to the commitment to follow up an incisive reform of the state: he is able to revive democratic participation, to ensure efficiency and stability in the political system and to strengthen public ethics. Moreover, the process of European integration obliges Italy, if it wants to remain competitive, to increase efficiency, timeliness and decision-making capacity. The adherence to an average European standard must be the compass of many of our interventions, avoiding always access to the commonplace of an alleged Italian exceptionality. This applies to the reduction of the number of parliamentarians, as well as to the correction of the perfect bicameralism, with the entrusting of political trust to the Chamber of Deputies alone and the distinction between a legislative function and a control function between the two Assemblies. Then there is a problem that concerns the assembleristic drift of our parliamentary system, of which the regulatory obstruction of these hours by the 5 Star Movement is tangible sign, recalling an old and very old politics. The Commission's ordinary powers must be strengthened in order to avoid the continued use of emergency procedures, such as decree-laws, major amendments and trusts. All this, not only harms Parliament's constitutional prerogatives, but produces a legislative avalanche of clauses and codes, with which we confront each other every day, which undermine the clarity of relations between the State and citizens, increasing the discredit of the Government and Parliament. Finally, there is a problem of stability, which cannot be eluded, because without stability the function of representation fails, as much as the ability to decide, and institutional discredit grows. This requirement accompanies as a ghost the constitutional history of the Republic from the time of the agenda of Thomas Perassi of 4 September 1946, on the basis of which it was adopted: " (...) the parliamentary system to be regulated, however, with constitutional arrangements suitable to protect the needs of stability of government action and to avoid the degenerations of parliamentarism." Little has been done since then, both on the side of a greater incisiveness of the reform of parliamentary regulations, which would have avoided the need for today's constitutional intervention, and for undoubted historical reasons: during the Cold War, the instability of governments was paradoxically fueled by the stability of the world geopolitical context that prevented the exchange and a normal dialectic with the opposition; in the last twenty years, due to a muscular bipolarism, founded on the reciprocal delegitimation of the enemy and characterized by a practice of the majority devoid of the necessary political and civil culture. This allowed for the first time the alternation of forces in the government, but provided that weak majorities were stabilised, because they were poorly cohesive, not formed on common perspectives and programs, but on the personal follow-up of the individual leaders. We have thus had parties, parties and majorities able to gather consensus, but not to express governability. The complexity of the problems to be faced and the good reasons for the system that lead us to do so make almost surreal and certainly not up to the current Italian political and institutional crisis the ideological opposition, too often malicious and poisoned, that seems to dominate the public debate, among the intellectual defenders of the Constitution as it is and those who want to upset it. It's not like that. I stress this point because the other point on which we are called today is the strengthening of Article 138 of the Constitution. As a Democratic Party we have chosen this main road, which guarantees not only the full sovereignty of Parliament, but also the involvement of every citizen. This reform process will end in a confirmed referendum even if a two-thirds majority is reached in Parliament. As I have already said in this House, the new elements of this decision do not escape us, but let us consider the derogation, which we reaffirm today, from Article 138 justified by a higher common good which binds us before our national community. We need a frank commitment and free from ideological prejudices to involve and inform the greatest number of citizens, not only with the precious tools of the network that have also been activated by the Government, but also with the mobilization of parties, which must organize information and listening campaigns. Last Sunday I was in Trevinano, a small hamlet between Lazio, Umbria and Tuscany, and to my amazement I found myself in front of a hundred citizens eager to know and understand what is happening. There are no friends of the 5 Star Movement, citizens of the A series, those who write to you and who all too often exploit your interventions, and those who instead meet the Democratic Party around Italy in its daily political activity. Also thinking of them, the small community of Trevinano, I conclude by confirming the Democratic Party's vote in favour of the establishment of the Parliamentary Committee for Constitutional Reforms: to restore confidence in the institutions of the Republic and make Italy more efficient and competitive. [[Applause from the PD Group.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u101	Senator Palermo is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u102	Mr President, I would like to thank the Minister very much for the report he has presented to us, which is full of ideas and possible solutions to the institutional deadlock in which this country stands. It is clear that the report, being a complex text, has something that is acceptable and others less, but the aim was to stimulate reflection and this objective has certainly been achieved. I have to say that criticism of this review process mainly focuses on the method, but it seems to me that the method is the strongest, most appreciable aspect, also because so far we have only reached this point and the content will come. It is appreciated for the degree of pluralism that for the first time is introduced in this way in a constitutional revision: there had never been before the case of an academic stimulus as varied as this report of the sages, a public consultation of this kind made by the Government, the decision - it should be remembered - that remains firmly anchored in the Parliament's classrooms and the preparation of the work made by a small group, composed of members of the merit committees that should also be the most qualified to do so. It is clear that if the whole of this House had taken care of it, nothing would have been achieved. There is also a constituency of areas of intervention: a new Constitution is not being written, perhaps this should be repeated. The question of Article 138 is not really convincing, because it is a one-off derogation, but above all a procedural one, which serves to strengthen and not weaken the guarantees laid down in Article 138, which, incidentally, are not particularly strong. Our Constitution, among the rigid ones, is one of the least rigid. This is a path to stiffen it a little bit more, let's say so. There will be a de facto mandatory referendum, even if the reform is approved by a two-thirds majority, for example. On the contrary, I would say that even the obstructionism that we are experiencing today shows that we need to adapt this Constitution, by introducing, for example, an adequate statute for opposition, which is lacking today. This is a problem, because the opposition has no other instrument than to lengthen the times, to try to have more time, but in the end from the point of view of practical incisiveness there is little. In short, amidst many difficulties we have managed to find the right path and this thanks also to the good work of the Minister and President Finocchiaro, whom I take advantage of to thank. I would like to focus on just two issues, among the many dealt with in the report, which are connected: one is the bicameral structure and the other is what is defined - with an expression that I personally do not like very much - the form of State. It was already used in the Bicamerale D'Alema, but this terminology remains a problem, it would be better to talk about relations between the state, regions and local authorities. As for the bicameral arrangement, the need to overcome the perfect bicameralism is established, but, having done so, we will have to think more about what to differentiate and how to do it. In fact, the problem is essentially the relationship of trust between the Senate and the Government. Everyone agrees, I hope also in this House, that this report will have to be removed, but then the opinions on the role of the second chamber are still completely open and we will have to think about it in the Committee. The prevailing opinion in the work of the Government Commission is that of a Senate with mixed composition, i.e. largely elective but with a legal presence of the Presidents of the Region. It seems to me that an option of this kind would create a system that is neither meat nor fish and would not solve what in doctrine was called Madison's paradox, that is, that for which the Senate is either elective or territorial, but the two things together hardly go. If it is elective (and does not matter if it is directly or indirectly elected), the political criterion inevitably prevails over the territorial one. Therefore, if you want a territorial Senate, this must not be elective and therefore cannot be a political House. Therefore, the House of Regions, which everyone is talking about, risks being an empty slogan of content. We'll think about it. Halfway solutions are perhaps potentially problematic and it does not seem entirely peregrine the hypothesis, advanced by some members of the Commission, of even considering a single-chamber arrangement, constitutionalizing the Conferences and introducing them into the legislative circuit for some subjects. But of course there will be a way of discussing this in depth in the Committee. The second and final point concerns the question of Title V. All the members of the Commission - and we do not need to be particularly wise to realize this - agree on the poor performance of the system introduced by the 2001 reform, because it has brought little clarity, a high conflict between the State and the Regions and the fact that many regions have either forced a little too much the constitutional limits or, much more often, have shown excessive prudence or, for various reasons, have not exploited the areas of competence that even the reform acknowledged them. To make things worse, as we know, the new financial reports introduced by Article 119 have been implemented for a very long time and then, when finally the law on so-called fiscal federalism was passed in 2009, everything was overwhelmed by the economic emergency, the implementing decrees got bogged down and therefore remained essentially firm. That the system should be profoundly changed and that some basic competences should be transferred back to the State (e.g. the large transport networks, which are a matter of national interest as well as the coordination of communication, ports), is certainly a welcome aspect. However, it remains to be understood - this report does not yet say so - in which direction we want to go. The solution depends on the analysis of the problem that is being done, the therapy depends on the diagnosis and many, unfortunately also among the tests, seem to think that regional autonomy is the problem, when it seems to me instead that it is the solution. If we are going to reform, I would say, Title V, but thinking that we need to recentralise the system, we risk curing evil with the wrong medicine and making the problem worse instead of solving it. This applies also and above all to special autonomy, which are often seen as exceptions to be tolerated, not to be touched and, where possible, deleted. But it should not be forgotten that these represent a phenomenon that affects 20% of the regions, 15% of the population and 25% of the national territory. These regions can and should be the driving force behind decentralisation for all, rather than being seen, and I quote one of the reports: 'Now, seventy years after the end of the war, which is largely unjustified, only a source of unacceptable inequalities' (an argument that does not seem very legal). In these years, as we know, instead of making the ordinary regions more special, we have tried to make the special regions more ordinary, and I do not think that the results have been particularly brilliant. We will have to think about this too. But the basic point where you will want to go is then about the instruments with which to implement this review. In the Commission, the assumption that it is necessary to go beyond the catalogues of the subjects in order to introduce general areas and policies, instead of detailing the specific competences of the State and the Regions, seems to prevail. The intention is certainly to simplify, and it is commendable, but the consequence in this way risks being easily a centralization of competences, because it is evident that the wider and more generic the scope, the easier it is to identify a prevailing national interest that inevitably attracts the single function to the center. It seems to me more in line with the intention of a decentralization that functions in the opposite direction, namely to specify much more in detail the competences and to identify more clearly who does what. This, not by chance, is the direction in which we have gone in the most recent reforms that have affected other European states. I am thinking of the revision of the German Basic Law in 2006, of the new Spanish Statutes, and even of the United Kingdom, where a more precise division of competences is being codified in recent legislation. But all these are reflections for the work that awaits us. We must now start and then constructively discuss the contents and, as the Minister said, thank you: we cannot fail, let us try! [[Applause from the Aut Group (SVP, UV, PATT, UPT) - PSI-MAIE]]

ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u107	Senator Mazzoni is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u108	Mr President, first of all, I would like to thank Mr Quagliariello for being here despite the obvious indisposition. Those who have good memory remember that the past legislature was born as a constituent, in the name of mutual legitimacy, and we saw how it ended. It ended without producing any reform, just like the Bicamerale D'Alema, as the Commission presided over by Nilde Iotti and as the one entrusted, even before, to the liberal Aldo Bozzi. The fact that we are discussing institutional reforms together today is therefore already a good thing in itself. First of all because it means that the Constitution is no longer an untouchable totem, as well as the ultimate garrison of democratic freedoms, as, on the other hand, a current of thought continues to hold. The truth must be said here: if in the last legislature, for the umpteenth time a spider has not got out of the hole on reforms, it is also for a cultural, political and profoundly undemocratic attitude of certain elites, according to which the electoral majority acquired by the People of Freedom and the League did not have a full legitimacy because it considered an expression of a people who does not respond to that general interest established by a narrow circle of enlightened people who systematically use the politicized part of the judiciary as head of rams. It is no coincidence that a public prosecutor in Palermo even theorized the suspension of democracy to defend it from the will of the people. On the basis of this clearly unacceptable premise, any proposal to amend the current constitutional balances has become so unworkable. Yet the Italian Constitution must be revisited is also a fact of historical evidence, because it was born of a forced compromise, since Italy had to deal with the strongest Communist Party in the West, and it is not by chance that it has been discussed for some time whether it is right that the Italian Republic should remain founded on work, as its first article states, and not on freedom, like all other Western democracies. But the anti-reformer party remained strong, pretending not to understand that the Constitutions are not intangible, and not to know that the constituent Fathers themselves in their farsightedness had left the door open to future changes. However, the current constitutional structure, partly replaced by the material Constitution that actually led to the direct election of the Premier, has not strengthened its powers, just as it is necessary to be blind to not see that the perfect bicameralism is a ball at the foot of the country and that the reform of Title V of the Constitution, approved in 2001 by the center-left, must be completely revised. Regarding the powers of the Government, it seems to me very significant - as Senator Gotor has recalled - that celebrating the 60th anniversary of the Constitution, President Napolitano has made an almost literal reference to the agenda Perassi, with which the constituent Assembly pronounced itself for the adoption of the parliamentary system, to be disciplined, however, with devices suitable to protect the needs of stability of government action and to avoid degenerations of parliamentarism. This is to recall a weak point that was well present in the minds of the Constituents, namely the insufficient guarantee of the stability of government action, a luxury that no country can afford anymore and that was effectively addressed - it must be recognized - in the Violant draft of the last legislature remained then, unfortunately, dead letter. Therefore, on the one hand there is the awareness that our Constitutional Charter must be adapted to the times, at least in its second part; on the other hand, there is the resistance to all things that continue to mythize it. However, a constitution that has produced 56 governments in 60 years is clear that it must be changed, starting with the very part relating to the powers of the state and constitutional relations, because we can no longer afford an assembly democracy and not a ruler. It is useful to recall, in this regard, that the centre-right constitutional reform had done so in 2006. It was not perfect, but it was a step forward, as it contained the strengthening of the powers of the Premier, the reduction of parliamentarians and some corrective to the crazy reform of Title V which led to a perennial dispute between the State and the Regions. The left, however, had her rejected in the confirmation referendum. Experience has taught us, since the 1990s, that the reforms of the electoral system, although necessary, are not enough to achieve stable governing democracies. We must therefore start with the form of the State, as Mr Quagliariello rightly pointed out. The Committee of Experts has presented Parliament with a report which prefigures two hypotheses, preferably, for the first: that of a Parliamentary Government of the Prime Minister, with a President of the Republic as a guarantee, in which the powers of the Premier are strengthened, with the possibility of appointing and withdrawing Ministers and, above all, of dissolving the Chambers. Then, finally, the perfect bicameralism disappears. The other is that of semi-presidentialism to French, which was proposed by Berlusconi and Alfano and voted at first reading by the Senate in the last legislature, but to which the PD opposed pervicaciously. A form of government that in France ensured stability and governability, as was demonstrated last year, given that the direct election with the ballot between the first two candidates was the driving force behind the successive legislative elections and ensured the governability even in the presence of a strong crisis of the political system, with the anti-system forces that in the first round had achieved 40% of the votes. The polar stars of the reform must be two: a stronger executive and the maintenance of bipolarism, because any reform that favored a neocentric solution would be unacceptable, and on this point fortunately the Minister has said quite clearly that we do not go back. The reform of the second part of the Constitution is therefore the indispensable tool for modernising the institutions and to equip our country with the same tools that other Western democracies have for a long time, so as to address on a level playing field the great challenges that international competition imposes on us. The new electoral law will then have to be, and I stress then, a consequence and complementary to institutional reform. Of course, Minister, there is a gap that looks a lot like a black hole in the reform that we are about to discuss in Parliament. In fact, once again, the accounts are not made with attempts, often successful, to destabilize the executive power through the political use of justice that have dramatically characterized the last twenty years of Italian political life. There would be an urgent need for targeted constitutional changes, which also intervene on the imbalance of powers between the judiciary and politics in order to escape once and for all from the climate and culture of the years of Tangentopoli for which requests for authorization to proceed are transformed into piazza processes and the notices of guarantee are equivalent to as many preventive convictions. The frightened politics bowed to the politicized fringes of the judiciary and the modification of Article 68 of the Constitution has resulted in a very serious vulnus in our system of guarantees. The Berlusconi case is the smoking test, even if too many people in this House turn to the other side for political convenience. I listened yesterday, with dismay, to Senator Casson's claim that Berlusconi's decadence does not require a secret vote, because it would not be a vote on the person but a matter of "cleaning" - and I stress this term - that the Senate must do by expelling from itself a sort of virus. A thesis in my opinion aberrant, which shows how the Jacobine justiceism still alligni in the belly of the Italian left. On the other hand, it would be necessary to try to return to the Constitution, and it is remarkable that this requirement is not felt by those who have always stood up to clothe themselves with the intangibility of the Charter, because the original Article 68 was not a coercion of the principle of equality of citizens in the face of the law, but an implementation and a guarantee of Article 67 on the freedom of the parliamentary mandate. Under these conditions, however, it is clear that there is no political space to do so, even if PdL and PD are together with the government of the country. A real paradox, allow me. But be careful: today it was Berlusconi, yesterday it was Mastella's wife, tomorrow it will be someone else's turn, if the right balance between politics and the judicial order is not restored. That, just what the constituent Fathers had predicted. PRESIDENT. - The debate is closed. Senator Girotto is enlisted to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u109	Mr President, it is customary to say that the Republican Constitution is the result of a "compromise" between the political forces represented in the Assembly and in particular between the Catholic-inspired forces and the Communist and Socialist groups. This statement might surprise those who looked at all the political events of the period 1946-1947, with regard to the ordinary themes that in those years came to prominence and marked deep divisions between the same parties represented in the Assembly. The relations between the main political forces were significantly changed over the course of that two-year period. On the international political side, the context was certainly not the easiest: at the time when the Assembly exercised its functions, the Italian Government was engaged in the difficult, almost impossible, negotiation on the conditions of the Peace Treaty. Moreover, on the side of internal politics there were the first marked oppositions between the political forces that had supported the first governments after the armistice of 1943. During the period of activity of the constituent assembly, there will be four different governments, in fact, united by the figure of the President of the Council, in the person of Alcide De Gasperi. The real break-up is following De Gasperi's trip to the United States, followed by the decision to exclude communists and socialists from the government. The fracture is, at that time, insanable and drives leftist forces to a marked opposition, which also results in opposition to the ratification of the Peace Treaty. On this and on other issues of internal and international politics, the dialectic between government and opposition was lively, but the discussion on the draft Constitution left in the background the contingent political oppositions, which in fact did not condition it in a decisive way. Among the reasons for this phenomenon, which in our time could be considered an anomaly, a authoritative constitutionalist (Cheli) has identified the government's decision not to participate formally in the work of drafting the Constitution, without prejudice to the participation of De Gasperi and other Ministers, in a personal capacity; the secrecy of the work carried out by the Commission of the 75 and sub-committees, and above all by the drafting committee that prepared the text to be discussed in the Assembly; more importantly, the "holding" of the fundamental values on which the agreement had formed between the forces that led the Resistance to fascism: unity, Risorgimento tradition and, above all, antifascism. The elements mentioned above have therefore facilitated the attainment of an agreement, which has in any case taken longer than those anticipated (so that the duration of the functions of the Assembly has been extended for three times): the existence of such an agreement, combined with the opposition between the forces of government and those of opposition already recorded outside the Assembly, has led to the use of the term "compromise" as a formula that would sum up the underlying philosophy that animates the choices of the Constituents. On the subject of the organization of the State, there was a wide confluence on the supremacy of Parliament and on the configuration of the President of the Republic as a guarantee body; the parliamentaryism of the system found, moreover, compliance with the approval of an agenda (presented by the deputy Perassi) aimed at promoting the introduction of instruments of "rationalization" to prevent both a drift in the sense of assembly, and an excessive exaltation of the role of the Government and of the President of the Council. It is certain that, in the face of some problems on which it might have been impossible to reach an immediate agreement, the Constituents preferred to postpone the choices to a subsequent implementation discipline: it seems emblematic the reference to the Constitutional Court, where the examination of the few articles of the Constitution dedicated to it gives the impression of how uncertain the reflections of the Assembly characterized. In addition to these "recommendation" decisions, which we might consider strategic, or in any case necessary, there are many constitutional rules that need to be implemented by the ordinary legislator; I repeat: implemented by the ordinary legislator. This fact, in many respects, is consistent with the very nature of the constitutional text, intended to contain rules of principle, which give Parliament the choice of how, subsequently, to implement the principles expressed in the Charter. The doctrine mentioned at first pointed out that the Constitution was born "presumed" in the sense that it had appeared from the beginning little in harmony with the political balances of the time, but it had largely anticipated subsequent developments, thanks also to a formulation of constitutional provisions that favored an elastic interpretation of the same, as I will shortly point out. Certainly, with regard to some institutions, the time of implementation of the Constitution was very long: only in 1956 the Constitutional Court began to operate; only in 1958 the Supreme Council of the judiciary was established; only since 1970 the regional order was made operational. And again: for the approval of the referendum law it had to wait until 1970; the first law on the regulation of the strike - it seems incredible - dates back to 1990 (45 years of delay!) and in the same year a law on the system of local autonomy was passed for the first time. On the grounds of this slow implementation, it is easy to identify in the political circumstances the common denominator of all the implementing choices, insofar as the implementation of this or that institute was accelerated or delayed depending on the conveniences of the majority political forces and the greater or lesser pressure of the opposition forces. I mentioned earlier the peculiar characteristic of constitutional norms, which helped to strengthen the current situation of the Constitution and encouraged its resistance to changes in the historical, political, social and economic context. In at least one case, of extraordinary importance for the impact it has had on the system of sources, and therefore on our legal system, it seems that it can be said that the Constitution has undergone an implicit transformation, even if it is not the subject - until 2001 - of a constitutional revision intervention according to the forms provided for in Article 138. The concept of "constitutional transformation" has been developed by American scholar Bruce Ackerman - to be then taken up by our doctrine and adapted to our order - and moves from the consideration for which, at some moments in republican history, the interpretation of constitutional norms has undergone a radical change, and however endorsed by the Supreme Court: just think, for example, of the legislation wanted by Roosevelt in the New Deal, clearly oriented to an increase of the powers of the federal state in the management of the economy, to the detriment of the powers of the Member States and sometimes in open contrast with the principles of the free market, until then proclaimed and protected with particular emphasis by the Supreme Court. Is such a phenomenon also found in our constitutional system? The answer seems to be yes, at least if one analyses the problem of the integration between the Italian legal system and the Community legal system. Those who sought in the text of the Constitution before 2001 an explicit reference to the European Union, or simply to Community sources, would inevitably have been disappointed: the constituent Assembly had acted at a time when European integration seemed above all a long-term wish, while accession to the United Nations was more concrete and imminent. However, I believe that no one can doubt the importance that Community rules have taken on board in our legal system, not since 2001, but at least for a few decades. Indeed, it can be argued that entire sectors are now almost exclusively governed by Community rules, but this can already be said for several years. I could safely go on, in the ten minutes I have left, with these very well-educated and brilliant speeches, which, of course, I did not write, but a teacher of constitutional law. However, since we are very close, I believe that I can speak in a much more pragmatic way, starting from the fact that most of the laws are now being written in Brussels. In this respect, I would like to give the Italians first information that every day there are 1,500 financial lobbyists in Brussels who go near the various offices and departments to push the legislation in such a way that it corresponds to the wishes of the banks. As you know, the lobbyist's job in Brussels is absolutely regulated and registered, in order to do so it is necessary to register, specifying who are stakeholders. Here we are starting from the assumption that we are in democracy, but I think we are making a huge mistake, if we think that Parliament is the place where laws are made. We are not, in fact, under the regime of democracy, but of "denarocracy." There is no country in the world where democratic principles are fully implemented. From what world is the money ruler world. But then, do we decide or do we decide the big finance? After the financial crisis of 2008, the European Union itself ordered a Commission - specifically a Directorate-General - to carry out an analysis to understand what had happened and what had been the causes of that collapse. The Commission has written a report, available on the Internet, in which it makes it clear that the legislative function has been delegated to private business officials. A very elegant diction to say that the laws write directly to you by the officials of the banks and the insurances. We are here discussing how to amend a Constitution, because we think we are in democracy, but in reality, as I have already said, much of our way of acting is determined in Brussels, where regulations and directives are written by bank and insurance officials. Then I am very happy that we are in four cats to hear it, because of course these are news that must remain between four cats, it is news that must not be written. We continue to think that Parliament is the place where we decide on the destinies of Italy. But Parliament is not the seat of Italy's destinies. Decisions are made outside these classrooms. In fact, I am in this Chamber as little as possible; I stay in the office and call to the right and to the left, because the agreements are taken out of here. The agreements are made between the trade associations, between the producers' associations, between the various lobbies, which rule here but are out of here. Therefore, the 5 Star Movement will continue to be present in the Chamber. As you can all see, however, even when the talk gets uninteresting we are always a relative majority. To the 5 Star Movement goes the great merit, which perhaps you have not yet understood, of being deeply rooted among the population. We are constantly meeting the basis of the company, whether employees are, above all, the engine of the country or - as you all know - the very small enterprises, the craftsmen. We have been meeting daily for months with the craftsmen, who are those who keep Italy standing. You may not even understand that you can make all the laws you want, but if you don't give them the chance to work, the reform will be done soon, in the sense that there will be no more money to pay anything and then everything will fall down. About raising VAT from 21 to 22% any craftsman with fifth grade asks me who is the politician who made this silly sound, when we all know that tax revenue will decrease. The goal was to raise it, but the opposite will actually happen, as already demonstrated by the previous transition from 20 to 21 percent. So this decision wasn't even a jump in the dark. I repeat, we know very well that tax revenues will decrease. So the reflection that I do, in a few intimates, is that we don't count anything in here. We don't count anything in here. We can have all the jackets and ties of this world, we can go around the corridors with a very diplomatic and very professional attitude, but the decisions, even those concerning the economy and finance, are taken out of here. Here we are talking about a critical issue in the fact that we are in a multi-level system of government between the European Union, the State and territorial autonomy, which is much more complex and articulated than in the past. This system, therefore, needs to be carefully calibrated in order to safeguard the framework of the fundamental values and principles of the Constitution of 1947. Perhaps in this case too we must carefully consider the word "safeguarding"; my vocabulary must be updated, and not only my vocabulary because it is not "safeguarding," but it is "scarding" itself. These are words that always start with the letter "s" but they are different. In essence, the problem is that, at the time of the elections, a stable majority cannot be created. But it is not the Constitution's fault that the people who have the most choices divide between them. At best you could say - and indeed you say so - that it is the fault of the related electoral law (for this reason you make the connection). We have come back to the point that we have to make an electoral law cut out so that only one wins: let's say so! Everyone thinks that they change the electoral law by believing that they win, instead of asking themselves the problem of making an electoral law that is as fair as possible: this is the purpose of an electoral law. It must represent the electorate's intentions fairly and correctly without distortion. On this, which is a mathematical subject, there are fine treaties and fine paradoxical examples that show how one can distort the use of an electoral law. Since later there are very strange elucubrations and elaborations of electoral laws, I say to you that the electoral law that best reflects the electoral voting intentions is the proportional one without barriers and without majority prizes. It is an electoral law that has worked well for over 30 years. Then more decision-making electoral law attempts were made, with the result that it did not improve, but the system got worse. So the more you try to make an electoral law that goes in the sense of creating a majority that commands and not that governments - it's a non-subtle distinction - and less successful in this effect. Paradoxically it was better before when there were five parties. All in all, even this lack of decision-making, which is constantly indicated in all these nine pages, is not the fault of the Constitution. Who decides, in fact? The Executive: it's called the Executive, the Government. If the government is not able to decide, it must choose between two things: decide or go home! If you can't do what you're in the government for, make room for someone else who can. It is not the fault of the electoral law if you are unable to decide; it is not the fault of the Constitution either. It's because you're trying to put mixed interests together. The Constitution must not be a mass of granite that cannot be shaped and splintered; nor must it be a flexible joint that bends with every breath of wind. It must be, it should be, it would like to be a kind of flexible steel that you manage to remould tirelessly under the action of fire and under the action of the hammer of a strong and conscious worker! Let's see if the planned arrangements can approach this ideal. First of all, we wanted the revision initiative to be the responsibility of both the Government and each individual Member. We wanted the process of the two readings, with an interval of three months between one and the other, because something as serious as constitutional reform is not the product of momentary and demagogic impulses or, however, not well confirmed and meditated. We called for the absolute majority (half plus one of the members of the two Chambers) to avoid minority handstrokes, always deplorable, dangerous, very dangerous in the supreme constitutional matter. In addition to these guarantees, which seem to us to be very serious and substantial, we have introduced another one which can reassure everyone: the recourse to the very source of sovereignty, namely the popular referendum, when one fifth of the members of a House, five hundred thousand voters or five regional councils apply for it. With this also the rights of the minority, of a small minority, are effectively protected, while the appeal to the people remains open, even by a comparatively small part of the public opinion. There was still a need to protect legal certainty. This has been done by setting a time limit within which the appeal can be promoted: three months. Three months seem long enough for public opinion to set in motion, if necessary, and appear short enough not to leave too much of the constitutional rules undefined. The use of the referendum is excluded in one case only, when the law has been passed in both Chambers (with four votes, since the double-reading system is envisaged) by a majority of two thirds of the members, a majority that is really highly qualified. Is this rule appropriate? It seems to us, because it had regard to the concrete political reality, to the concrete political landscape of our country. We believe in the Constitution of the Italian Republic.
