ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u128	Senator Romani Maurizio is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u129	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the measure we are discussing, the constitutional bill establishing a parliamentary committee for constitutional and electoral reforms, clearly represents an extraordinary procedure, a derogation from Article 138 of the Constitution. In a rigid Constitution like ours, the procedure for constitutional revision is in fact only the one provided for in the Constitution itself. A constitutional law either complies with Article 138 or is different: there is no tertium genus. Nor can it be objected that, this time, constitutional law would foresee that it would be subsequently passed by the people. If - as it is hoped - the procedure in place is not "constituent" (i.e. having a radically contrary purpose to the current constitutional order), this implies that the sovereignty of the people must also be exercised, in this regard, in the forms and within the limits of the Constitution. Therefore, the people will not be able, with the subsequent referendum vote, to eliminate the procedural vices that affect the constitutional law. On the other hand, the constitutional law under discussion does not aim to modify the constitutional revision process by allowing, for the future, scholars, instead of politicians (I would say unfortunately), to devise constitutional solutions, parliamentary committees to draft the text of constitutional laws and the people to always make their voice heard with mandatory referendums. Rather, the constitutional law under consideration provides for a derogation from Article 138. But a derogation that explains stable consequences for the future on the form of government, on the relationship between Parliament and government, on bicameralism, etc., what else is it, if not a surreptitious violation of the Constitution? Article 138 is that provision that has always guaranteed us against the blows of the majority of the moment, a perhaps fictitious majority, a deviant product of this unworthy electoral law. The President of the Constitutional Court himself argued that this Parliament was elected by a constitutionally flawed law. So I ask myself: what legitimacy does this Parliament have to take over the right to break down, even if only temporarily, one of the lintels of our system? Who guarantees us that derogation from Article 138 does not involve the constitutional upheavals from which our constituent Fathers wanted to shelter us? This Parliament? That is why the derogation from Article 138 is by no means merely a matter of procedure, but is substantial. The lack of political legitimacy, and in particular moral legitimacy, of this Parliament should have led its members to become more humble and consequently to merely intervene on what really needs to be done in the immediate future: a new electoral law, which would allow the election of a Parliament that could morally take responsibility for redesigning the constitutional architecture of our system. Not only is this right imposed on us, but it is claimed to do so by way of derogation from one of the most important provisions of the Constitution. The true Constituent, the only one we had, in the period 1946-1947, is far from the Fake Constituent, which we want to do now. A great work of constitutional literacy (there was a special ministry, run by Nenni, both with the Parri Government and with the De Gasperi Government), was added to the work of the Real Constitution. Daily broadcasts were broadcast on the radio, in which he was educated and informed. It was about involving as many people as possible in the draft Constitution. It is now a question of keeping it as hidden and as far away from public opinion as possible. It is also worth making a quick reference to the timing that this Parliamentary Committee on Reforms should follow: the draft Constitutional Law must be examined within 18 months of the entry into force of the draft Constitutional Law that we are examining and, within six months of the first sitting, the Committee will have to forward to the Presidents of the Chambers the drafts examined. Within three months of the date of transmission, each Chamber must complete the examination of the text and between the two deliberations there must be an interval not less than 45 days in place of the three months provided for in Article 138, while the need for approval by an absolute majority of the members of each Chamber is confirmed. This compression of the times, having given the Government a privileged amending power, the impossibility for individual parliamentarians to submend the proposals of the Government or of the Committee, the prohibition for parliamentarians in dissent with their Groups to submit their own amendments, the possibility of extending the revision to other articles of the Constitution where there is a connection with the Titles of Part II attributed to the competence of the Committee, the derogations provided for in the Regulations of the Chamber and Senate, the lack of attention to the publicity of the Committee's work: here, all this gives us the dimension of how there is a clear will to reduce the prerogatives of the individual parliamentarians and of the Parliament as a whole, mortifying its functions. I wonder how we Members can accept such humiliation. There are crucial issues at stake in our lives, not cold constitutional engineering operations, as we want to be led to believe. Work, equality, justice, everyone's rights, culture, health, legality and transparency: possible things in democracy when it expands, difficult or impossible when it shrinks. And on this point it should be emphasized that, if until recently the debate on constitutional reforms was divided into a dialectic between a government force and an opposition, now - thanks to the Government of the technicians first and to the broad understandings then - the opposition is practically excluded. And it is not just a detail, given how diligently the results of the 2006 referendums and the various attempts made by citizens to amend the electoral law were taken into account by this Parliament, whether they were laws of popular initiative or collecting signatures for referendum questions. It also seems useful to consider that the political forces that make up this strange majority demonstrate day after day that they have a profoundly different idea of the Constitution and the guarantees it contains. How can one think that within this Committee all differences will fade away in order to leave the pace to a conscious and reasoned discussion leading to good constitutional reform? It is quite predictable that the demeaning debate we are witnessing every day in this House on the few issues that really affect the interests of the parties will be repeated in the same way. Article 5 of the draft law fortunately allows a request for a confirmation referendum to be made even if the text is approved by a two-thirds majority. But this particular aspect cannot reassure us, at least until a serious involvement of the public is initiated in the debate that is taking place in these classrooms, strong of the awareness that these are not the issues on which the interest of the vast majority of citizens is concentrated. It is clear that the Constitution must and must be amended to respond to changes in a changing society, but it is equally clear that any change can only be resolved within the procedure provided for in the Constitution itself. We do not want to delay the reforms, we simply want everything to happen in the perimeter of the Constitution, insisting on the need to give voice to the citizens. We have a previous example that must make us think, the amendment of Article 81 on budgetary balance: we did not want to pay attention to those who suggested not to approve it by a two-thirds majority, so that citizens could express themselves. And maybe - but it's just a suggestion! - before you look for weird ways to change the Constitution, you could start by actually implementing it. So, first, to implement it; then to adapt it to changes, but certainly not to upset it and, above all, not to demolish it. The Charter can be changed, but with great caution and broad consensus. Having said that, Madam President, since we have made some confusion, I will read what Senator Ivana Simeoni has written for her, since I still have time. So what I'm going to read now is Senator Simeoni's speech. Dear Senators and Senators, in 1947 our constituent Fathers wrote the Constitution. Characters like, for example, Piero Calamandrei. Today we are in this House to discuss whether or not to change one of the fundamental mechanisms provided for in the Constitution: the constitutional revision procedure.

ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u132	Senator Mussini is registered to speak. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u133	Madam President, I would like to thank Mr Quagliariello who, despite his poor health, is here with us. I will try to give him satisfaction because I want to take up the text of his report. In some respects, this report is an open text because, although I myself pointed out some of the concepts highlighted by my colleague Mr Martelli, I have to say that slightly different reflections were born in me, so I am grateful to her for having built a report that is also suitable for further study; on the other hand, repetita iuvant, then here are some colleagues who were not present before (and vice versa). The first aspect that I have also stressed is the fact that, compared to when it is said that the objective... was certainly not to prepare an organic proposal for reform, I have to say that excusatio non petita, accusatio manifesta. You yourself, in fact, at the time of the presentation of this report, issued a statement saying that the Government would make proposals for a reform of justice. I remember the debate we had in this House where we wanted to keep Title IV of the Committee's work out of the scope of competence, so now we are also moving forward with respect to what is said and not said in this first part. I do not want to retrace all the steps that my colleague has taken and I just use some points that I want to bring to your attention to show how the first and most serious aspect of what we are doing now is that, through a transformation, we are intervening on something that has nothing to do with the frame, but with the contents and with the people who are actually in this frame. The text states: "The weakness of our institutions, conceived at a very different historical stage from the current one, is not alien to the difficulties of the country, and you are progressively inadequate to cope effectively with the challenges arising from the changed world economic context, as well as those related to the process of European integration." I would therefore also like to take up what my colleague, Senator Chiti, has said (who is not there now), who is also President of the 14th Commission, to underline that the problem is not the institutions, it is not our Constitution that we have to change: it is the people who today interpret the parliamentary work that our Constitution entrusts us that they do not put themselves in a position to really work to face the challenges associated with the process of European integration. We know that there is an ascending stage in the formation of Community law, but it is ignored and not practiced. We know that since the Lisbon Treaty as parliamentarians we would have obligations and rights towards the European Parliament, and it is we who do not practice them. It is not, however, a problem of the Constitution; it is our problem. It is useless to hope that it will be the Senate of the future that will be interfaced with the European Union if we are not able to use the instruments we already have to interface properly, proactively and imperatively with the European Union, to which we have heard so many times in this House proclaims or of unconditional love or rejection; useless claims, since we are not able to use the instruments we have. Then we are inadequate, not the Constitution. In the same passage, shortly after the Minister, he uses another reference saying that "our country has found itself devoid of instruments up to the decisions to be taken in an increasingly competitive global context and in a European framework in which states, in addition to respecting the strict budgetary rules, are called to contribute to the definition of strategies for development and employment and to the implementation of public policies that have an increasing and pervasive impact on the lives of citizens and on the work of enterprises." I would like to stress that this is the exclusive monopoly of the Commission for months, for years, and this is being denounced in these classrooms. We have repeatedly heard complaints that all these choices, which should be the subject of a real debate and discussion, are actually submitted to us exclusively by means of government measures. Now everyone, individually, is ready to acknowledge the fact that it is in the Commissions that the debate really takes on the strength that my colleague, Senator Vacciano, recalled, and on which my colleague Mr Romani also insisted. All those reasons why, in any case, the debate on the Constitution and its possible transformations should also take place in those same forums and for the same reasons why there should also be a debate on strategies for development, employment and the implementation of public policies that have this increasing and pervasive impact on the lives of citizens. So, once again, the problem is not the Constitution as such, but how we misunderstand it and how we force what, instead, our Constitution tells us that we should do. Later it is said again that we must reform and it is explained why: "coming back in the inability to express in the long term a stable political direction and rooted in the consent of the social body one of the most important elements of criticality". Now, where could this consent be if there is no freedom to choose your candidate? Where's the wonder? It is obvious and natural that the problem, once again, is not a problem of the Constitution, but of an electoral law that has prevented it from giving specific indications about its candidate and, therefore, has derelict and removed the substance and the matter of the relationship between the representative and the person who elects it for the representation of their demands. Rightly, here we go on to say that, therefore, the problem is the crisis of the parties. And, once again, where does the party crisis come from? Always and in any case from the institutions; always and in any case from a Constitution that establishes how these institutions should behave, how they should live and what their purpose and structure should be. But it's not! This is a presumptuous statement, because the crisis of the parties is not determined by the ineffectiveness of the institutions, but by the ineffectiveness of the parties themselves. [[Applause from Senator Martelli.]] We are also here because there is a sidereal distance, which is perceived by the voters. However, we are representatives of that 25% of voters who, of course, have taken note of a sidereal distance between their own demands, their everyday lives and those who represented them. This should be the subject of self-criticism, considerations, and not a presumptuous search for other causes. I am a teacher and all this reminds me of what the students do when, when they do not get good results at school, they say that the fault is of the teacher, it is of the programs, it is of the principal, it is of the institution. This really seems to me to be the immature attitude of those who, failing to make considerations about themselves, failing to reach maturity in order to face a change, go to find in others the reason for their failure. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] Then I come to face another aspect. The report also says, with regard to the deficit, that it is necessary to clear the field from the illusion of being able to remedy the serious deficits of decision-making capacity, stability and representativeness with yet another intervention on the electoral system alone, or relying on the exclusive spontaneous dynamics of political parties. Then there is the question of bipolarism. Now, I would also like to take up what Mr Gotor and Mr Palermo have said about what is happening in this House as a form of obstruction. I would like to invite you to take this thought. We are trying to bring to the attention the added value that can give an opposition that, unfortunately, is increasingly not considered. And here too I allow myself to refer to my professional activity. I try to explain to my students that democracy is not the existence of a majority that acts and governs. This is also due to the immaturity of those who interpret the majority, not only to an electoral law problem, to a problem of institutions: rather, I repeat, to a lack of maturity of those who interpret the majority. Listening to the minority is making decisions that are good for everyone, with respect, of course, to the difference in the representativeness of those who have a certain percentage compared to another. This is the sign of a democracy. To do the opposite, like what we see happening in this House, is not the sign of a democracy, but rather of a resistance to listening to others, and this too is not a problem of the institutions or the Constitution, but of the people and, perhaps, of the parties. As Senator Cervellini recalled, the signatures that have been collected are a sign. Here we have a "Senatus sine populo" (Senatus sine populo), and also this is not a problem of the Senatus as such, but it is a problem arising from the inability to relate to the people. The conclusions of the report refer to the consultation phase. However, rather than drawing experience from what is happening in the European Parliament, where a long, accurate and elaborate consultation phase is foreseen for the laws, here too, the consultation - as Mr Vacciano reminded us - is muddled and takes place in simplistic ways, so it is likely to be more like a fig leaf than a contribution from those whose signatures have been deposited. I do not want to bore my colleagues so much, but in recent months my reflection has also focused on something else. In fact, we have been hearing about reforms for months and, above all, the need to change the form. What I see, though, is that the substance is already deeply modified. It comes to mind then when I explain and tell my students what happened in 23 B.C., when Octavian began to pose the problem of linking the emptying of the Res publica - which he had already realized - with a new formal garment, which was to be attributed. It is at this point that my students ask me: "But how, the Senatus did not realize that it was actually the puppet of a profound transformation, which had already modified within the whole tradition of the Roman Public Res?." I answer no, first of all because, as we know, the Augustan historiographical has been cut off by the ability of Ottaviano to eliminate his detractors and all those who could think and urge a reflection on a problem of democracy and the inevitable passage that was taking place from the Res publica to the Principality. Then there is another reason. History teaches us that, when you live a historical moment, you hardly fully realize what you are experiencing, unless you are using the lenses of history and you are preparing to make a self-critical reflection on yourself. In this way we are faced with a semi-desert Senatus like today, which is indifferent, which does not see the problem that - in my opinion, but also in the opinion of other colleagues, and not only of the 5 Star Movement, because there have been many voices, even outside this House, that have been remembered during these two days of debate - considers instead as problems needs that many do not see. Perhaps I am witnessing a temporal hole in which I find myself living what I tell my pupils to have been lived by the senators who were around Octaviano and who did not realize that Octaviano was already Augustus, only that I do not see great Augustians on the horizon, at most I see some Caligula and perhaps some Nero. [[Applause from the M5S Group]] Mr Cervellini first recalled Seneca, who was a great interpreter of that moment, because Seneca was present, both as a politician and as a philosopher. And it is precisely Seneca - I want to remind you - to say at a certain point: "Vindica te tibi". I would like to say this: not the "Vindica te tibi" which leads to the end to withdraw and to move away from the challenge that this moment poses, but the "Vindica te tibi" which leads, instead, to being responsible, to giving a vow according to conscience and knowledge. To the limit, when you have to follow the indications of your own party, that the question of how this Committee is going to go ahead is whether there is a problem of the absence of a debate - as has been described by my colleagues - that the problem of so many citizens who ask questions and express their concern should be raised; and, even more so, that the problem of a Constitution which, in fact, has already been emptied of too many of its values has already remained a crust around us. ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u134	He's enlisted to speak Senator Della Vedova. You have the right to do so.
ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u135	Minister, I too would like to thank you for the attention and dedication with which you are following this debate and this debate which, in my opinion, takes place in a climate in which misunderstanding, suspicion and, in many cases, caricature dominate. I believe that we have a duty to clarify and explain why we support this process of constitutional reform. I have done so since before I was a Member of Parliament and I continue to do so today, for example, in the name of the many rightly mentioned Mr Calamandrei, and this is a tribute that I am very pleased with. That is, they are among those who have always thought that he was right in the discussion that took place in the Constituent on the form of government and that it was a sin that was later defeated. Calamandrei was right when, in his presidential position in favour of a strong government, he said that this would be the best antidote to populist and demagogic dictatorships. Instead, a different thesis prevailed: coming out of a dramatic phase of dictatorship in the name of the people who erased democracy, he chose to water down the powers of the government. What we are facing today are the knots that have come to the comb in the decades when the external constraints, which had made Italian democracy stronger, have, for one part, failed and for the other, have changed drastically; I think, for example, of the European constraint. I believe that we are dealing with those knots that came to the comb and that, as Calamandrei said, had to be resolved immediately with another vision. I do not believe that having a position of reform of the Constitution means certainly having more title to honor the memory not only of the constituent Fathers, but also of those, repeatedly recalled in the course of today's debate, who even gave their lives to get to that Constitution. I do not believe - as my colleagues will allow me - that it is an additional title to ask for in the name of those values of freedom, of guarantee and of the rule of law, to adapt the Republican Constitution, but - I say this with respect to the many moralists - it is certainly no less a title to be Italian citizens who honor this Republic, this Constitution and the history from which they originated. The whole process of constitutional revision, which we are discussing today, is regarded by many of its enemies as a kind of tampering with democratic guarantees and not - as I think it is - of proper maintenance of constitutional rules relating to the form of government and, more generally, the functioning of political institutions. There are authoritative representatives of the world of culture, including legal, who identify in the Constitution exactly as it is - and as they think it must remain - an embankment to political and civil degradation and describe the reforms outlined in the final report of the Commission for Reforms as a real risk to the political freedom of the country. I don't necessarily mean that thinking about changing these rules is a panacea. I hear and hear a lot of criticism from my fellow Members that you don't understand who you don't want to assume your responsibilities and the responsibilities of your failures, so you argue that the rules need to be changed. On the other hand, I think it would be useful to address, even if only in a doubting key, the fact that precisely the degradation which, according to them (I am referring to the defenders without doubt of this Constitution) has not been circumscribed, and instead has prospered exactly in the framework of constitutional rules that we have had for decades, so not only with us, but with the Italians who have been chosen from time to time by the voters. With reference to the alleged violation of Article 138 of the Constitution, which is seen as an intolerable tampering, this important and respectable part of Italian public opinion has always appealed to each of us senators not to vote for this reform - as I, on the other hand, wish to vote - by qualified majority in order to submit it to a referendum. As the Minister knows, my personal position and my group's position was that basically we could proceed directly by maintaining Article 138 as it is and that perhaps with this passage we risked overloading, even procedurally, the constitutional reform we are preparing for and that I hope it is not overwhelmed by crises that have little to do with the debate we are holding today. But, as a practicing referendary over the decades, and I do not regret it, I am aware that, thanks to the amendment of Rule 138 which we will vote on next week, it will be possible to submit to popular referendum the constitutional revision text possibly approved with 90% of the Members in both Chambers. Therefore, to these gentlemen who ask us not to vote by a two-thirds majority on the amendment of Article 138 in order to submit it to a referendum, I would point out that they say something wrong to the Italians and make propaganda: in fact, thanks to the amendment that we will approve - and that, of course, if we did not approve with a two-thirds majority it would be dropped - it will be possible to call for a referendum on a constitutional amendment voted by 99% of Members. Therefore, what we are doing - and I think this is the most qualifying point, on the rest perhaps we could proceed on an ordinary basis - is precisely to introduce a safeguard clause for popular sovereignty in relation to the constitutional revision that we are discussing. This, I believe, is important and an element of clarity with regard to Italian citizens, if we want to maintain loyalty and seriousness among ourselves in the debate. It has been said that constitutional conservatism does not exist. I know, respect, sometimes I understand, several times I do not understand the reasons of those who claim that the Constitution, Part II, must be unchangeable; I note that the reasons for this constitutional conservatism respond culturally and politically to the same conservative reflection that concerns the other reforms that I think the country needs and that, as far as I am concerned personally, over the decades, I have tried to implement, for example, through the referendary instrument; reforms that, at some happier times, in my opinion, of the government of Italy, have been undertaken, but that are rejected exactly on the basis of that intellectual and cultural obstacle to the changes provided they are. I believe that in this debate we must - and at least we, as a Group of Citizens' Choice for Italy, do so - have in mind the words of the President of the Republic, Giorgio Napolitano, at the moment when we have him - certainly not all of us, but the vast majority of us - called, certainly to the honour, but also to the burden of a second mandate, about the need to get to the end of a constitutional review process. This is precisely in the conviction, which is opposed to that of many colleagues, that these mechanisms, born at that precise moment, with that precise goal of the post-war and post-fascism, that also - not only, but also - in the procedures of our democracy now there are the reasons for the difficulties of the country and more generally of the decision-making process, which become difficulties of the Italian citizens. It's a challenge. We have different opinions on this matter, we try not to caricature each other's positions. I believe that there are elements - we live it from within - mentioned in the paper of the essays. On this I believe that here too - he did it very well yesterday, with punctuality - the Minister must recall the historical truth - and I use the tiny ones - of what happened. Nobody outsourced the constitutional revision, no one contracted the reform proposals. We have asked 40 non-parliamentary personalities (for free, since this seems to be a central theme today) and that they will certainly not become parliamentarians in this parliamentary term (our country is also made and strong of its culture and of the personalities who express it) to make available to Parliament their systematized reflections; after that, there is no constraint to use those reflections and proposals. In the same way, we can discuss the public consultation; that is, if it could be done better (as all things could be done better). I give the government credit for having done it, the public consultation. So, I think we need to discuss this. We will then have the opportunity to debate the substance of the proposals that will be made in Parliament (at that point, the proposals of the wise men will be literature, useful for the real, constitutional discussion of the procedure that we have chosen to undertake by amending Article 138, which - I repeat - will allow us to call to referendum the constitutional changes in any case). I believe that this is being discussed and that this is the subject. We do not do the caricature, if we really care, with passion, the institutions of the Republic, that we find ourselves, with honor, I think, of everyone, to serve. I want to close, Minister, with a consideration of the electoral law. The discussion and the plans overlap. We have decided - rightly - to choose two living parallels on the reform of the electoral law: the way, which I continue to hope and wish for is the main way of an electoral law that accompanies a constitutional revision that of an electoral law that responds, instead, to the commitment that Parliament has already assumed in some way to a modification of the current electoral law to the current Constitution that allows to overcome the objections. I am referring to the objections which, even if they were not put forward at the time of constitutionality by the Council (I do not know what the Constitutional Court would say about this), are now spread even - I believe - among the majority of us. I think that we must be ambitious in this regard; that we must not - that is - prepare a text which has only a minimal and defensive intention to respond to some of the more macroscopic objections to which the current electoral law has met. I am referring to the ambition of drawing up an electoral law which, in our expectations, has a minor effect, but in the same direction in which we want to go with the constitutional revision: to give simplicity, immediacy and more strength to the institutional and government system, even to the existing Constitution. We have proposed an electoral system that combines plurinomial colleges and a double coalition or list shift. We can discuss this, but we think that should also be the objective of revising the electoral law. If we believe in the ambition that we have set ourselves for the constitutional revision, it would, in my opinion, sound wrong if, on the other hand, we could not be ambitious even in this "temporary" passage of the revision of the electoral law to the Constitution in force. ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u137	Mr President, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to make some comments by bringing the reasoning plan to another level. I have read carefully the new pages on which Mr Martelli, Mr Mussini and others who have spoken on the subject have been working. And this is precisely in order to respect a very noble good, which is precisely our Constitution, which is not unchangeable, nor perpetual, but something that can be modified with extreme caution and that must be observed and evaluated with extreme humility, as someone with much more acumen than I myself, exactly Senator Tocci, said in the discussion carried out at first reading of the bill in question. I'd like to bring the plan of my reasoning to a pre-political level. I do so on the basis of those who have been able to reflect politically in these classrooms, in my opinion, very considerable: Aldo Moro. Moro, already an authoritative member of the Constitution and then of the Italian Parliament for several years, in 1948 was pleased that it had finally come to approve a system of rights and duties in which men of all currents - taken care of, of all currents - can meet, so wide is the vision of the world that inspires and understands the consideration of human needs that must be satisfied in social life. (...) But all the laws - Aldo Moro continued - are entrusted for their implementation to the social forces and to the moral conscience of the peoples, so that an orientation of solidarity and seriousness that is once given in a lucky historical conjuncture has to be preserved and strengthened by the vigilance of the social forces that have expressed it by themselves and by the permanent validity of the moral conscience of the whole society. Well, here we are talking about the vigilance that the social forces must put in place in order to protect a very noble good: the Constitution. There is talk of the permanent validity of the moral conscience of society all that must guard, as one watches over a small child, a newborn, someone who must be helped to grow so that it becomes stronger, stronger and can bear fruit. This reflection by Aldo Moro has called for teachings that I have heard on several occasions proposed by Gherardo Colombo when, explaining Article 1 of our Constitution, he reiterated that the concept of work, which we perhaps naively refer to a merely productive vision, must be understood differently. The work is that continuous effort, that attempt that every day every one of us, Italian citizens, must continue to deepen so that our Constitution comes to be something real, living, concrete, lived, rather than simply excited. I come to a criticism that I feel I have to do to the same Aldo Moro. We have all been taught that there is, there has been - and unfortunately it has been very deep - a gap between the formal Constitution and the material Constitution. If we are here today, if today we think that through an intervention on the institutions we can heal a crisis that is different, that is, of the parties, well it is because we did not understand how this gap was tragic and how this gap, not immediately healed, could lead to a very lively crisis, of which we are living today the consequences. However, since I am aware of my limitations, I stop here and invite you, for those few who are following me carefully, to reason on this fact: probably, in the opinion of many, not only of Italian constitutionalists but of foreign philosophers and theorists, we find ourselves one of the most beautiful Constitutions in the world; but we, unaware of this gift, have made it weird, with very limited awareness we have done harm, and now we find ourselves living without a tragic compass years. I have heard classicism quoted here - I must say it with great satisfaction, because from classicism you can only learn: when it is classicism it is because it is imperishable - and I was reminded of a famous phrase of St. Augustine: "Rome will not perish unless the Romans perish." I wonder if Italy has ever been born in function of that Constitution that has always and only been flagred, never taught, never made understood and above all never valued for how it should have been done. Even the fact that this debate, for the responsibility of all, takes place with a large part of these empty scrambles is a testimony of how little attention is paid to what, as Moro wanted, should be a foundation for our being together. ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u145	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, Senators, Undersecretaries, as is well known, the draft law of the Government we are debating, No 813-B, is fit to set up the Parliamentary Committee for Constitutional Reforms. The Government would therefore like to inaugurate a neo-constituent phase by way of derogation from the Constitution itself, which, in Articles 138 and 72, provides clearly and imperatively for the modalities of its revision. The neo-constituent government seems not to be more concerned with the limits and procedures of constitutional revision established by the constituent Fathers, as if they were laces and laces to the reformer (or counter-reformer) impetus so felt today. But it's not enough. This draft law is concerned, and is concerned, with greatly limiting the prerogatives of non-member Members of the Committee of the 42. For example, it will only be possible to table the amendments rejected by the Committee in the referee or new ones, but only if they are directly related to the modified parties. It will be possible to table amendments within five days of the date set for the start of the general discussion. These are just two examples of the additional limits that will have to be introduced to parliamentary activities for those who, like me, are outside this Committee. Now let us ask ourselves: what democratic legitimacy does the will to amend the Constitution that animates the Government and the majority support? The electoral law with which this Parliament was elected is probably spoiled by inconstitutionality (and the Constitutional Court will shortly establish it). In the absence of the preferred vote, parliamentarians were appointed by the party leaders and not by voters, with the exception of the internal primary (which we call "parliamentary") made by the 5 Star Movement. The parties that make up the current majority in the electoral campaign had sworn eternal enmity, with so much slogans: on the one hand, there were those who were stains and, on the other, those who spoke of "communists who eat children." Such a government has never been proposed to the citizens, to the voters, nor has it ever been discussed, and that is something that makes us think. Moreover, the current President of the Republic is in his second term: this practice has never been foreseen, nor has it been conceived by the constituent Fathers. With a Parliament, a Government and a Presidency of the Republic in this situation, which I merely define as exceptional, we would now like to create a further exception: a Committee for Constitutional Reforms to which we delegate the reform initiative of which it is the sole holder of law, and according to the Constitution, Parliament as a whole. Well, there is a limit to exceptions, to the right and to the abuse of power, and there is a limit to emptying the prerogatives of Parliament which exercises and must exercise popular sovereignty. Today we are all witnessing a tragic crisis of the legitimacy of democratic institutions. Never, in the history of the Republic, has confidence in Parliament and political forces been so low. Never, in the history of the Republic, have the institutions enjoyed such a poor and insufficient consensus: the voting percentages in the elections, in continuous decline, are the constant proof of this. And this is not because, as we hear repeated by many party leaders, the Italian people do not understand, do not appreciate the reformist efforts, or because the young Italians would be "unworkable," as they had the courage to declare recently a Minister, or because the Italians are "bambuccioni", "pigri", "accustomed to live above their own means." Such are the insults and contempt reserved to the citizens and the Italian people by the last governments. Well, if the confidence in politics is at the very least historical, it is not for these reasons, it is not because the Italians don't understand it or they are "bombs." It is because, on the contrary, the citizens understand all too well! It is because citizens understand very well the departure of politics from the pursuit of the common good, to draw far too close to particular and partisan interests: we could cite countless examples - we have seen them in this House - from slot machines to the purchase of F-35, to the TAV. And then, in this situation of democratic crisis, before we put in the field of intentional attempts at constitutional reform, we should bring politics closer to the citizens. We urgently need positive referendums, new and effective ways of consulting the people's will, procedures for participation from the bottom up in governance, the establishment of new places of confrontation and dialogue between citizens and institutions. In this the parties failed. From the square and the parade last Saturday, and even before from popular support to the initiatives of the 5 Star Movement in defense of the Constitution, a desperate scream of Italian citizens in defense of legality and an unavoidable request for participation has been lifted. We need more democracy, not derogations and exceptions to the rules laid down in the Constitution! The majority parties have long announced that they want to change nothing less than the form of State, from a parliamentary Republic to a presidential Republic. Allowed and not allowed that we are already, in fact, in a presidential Republic, I wonder if these parties have a vague feeling and feeling of what they are asking for. Who did they consult with to establish that we have to travel to a presidential republic? Who did they talk to before establishing that the priority of Italian citizens would be to break up the Parliamentary Republic? What legitimacy and consensus do they imagine to have in the face of such final choices? The whole process of constitutional reform, badly embroiled by the majority parties by way of derogation from constitutional rules, is without legitimacy. As a 5 Star Movement we demand that this process be interrupted, that the majority parties stop and reflect, that they consult the citizens before any further step. A few days ago the great popular demonstration in Rome, which I have already mentioned, declared that the Constitution is the main road. Let this Parliament not lose its way! ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u147	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, institutional reform and constitutional revision are important and challenging words, which require all of us, representatives but also citizens of this Republic, a careful and courageous reflection, a constant and determined commitment, the refusal of approximations, hasty announcements or noises, which are not suited to such a high and difficult task. Those who underestimated this passage would be deeply mistaken, that is to say they thought of it as a phase of the political battle of these years. It's a lot more than that. We must and we want to change rules and parts of our Constitution, of that solemn act which governs the fundamental rules within which the life of the Republic and of our democratic society takes place. A high and difficult task, in order to prepare ourselves to which we must respond to an issue that is before us, which is in the minds of the citizens, sometimes in a confused and unconscious way. Why do we need to change part of the Constitution? And again, what kind of change is necessary and right today for our country? These are fundamental questions, which do not allow a quick and simple answer, but require a research, a careful analysis of what has happened and has not worked in recent decades; they require a dialogue and a confrontation that is not preliminary and not contained in a partisan perspective, in a defense of old interests and positions. Allow me to make a first statement; our Constitution has not failed its objectives, it is not a Constitution to be thrown away. When, in March 1947, the constituent assembly began the general debate in this House, we warned, after the tragedies that had struck Italy and Europe as a whole, that some fundamental goods had to be assured to the country and to future generations: freedom and respect for the rights of the human person, popular sovereignty expressed by the freely elected Parliament, the political and moral unity of the nation, the social and civil process of our people. Well, this set of values and this fundamental heritage have been guaranteed to the Italians by the Constitution in recent decades, despite uncertainties and difficulties, persistent oppositions and terrible attacks on democracy, such as terrorism and then organised crime and the mafia, which today threatens and attacks our civil and political institutions. Why has our Constitution been able to cope with these challenges? Many factors have been involved and I believe that the strength of our people, its unity around democratic values, has been fundamental, but also - I repeat and want to stress this - the modernity and foresight of the Constitution and its general structure, which in the century of the great ideological and principle divisions, of which we all see today the crisis, perhaps irreversible, has been able to achieve a balance of values: freedom and solidarity, unity and pluralism, the indivisibility of the State and decentralisation, the right to work and freedom of enterprise, private property and public control of the economy. So let us not throw this Constitution away; we do not want a whole new Constitution. The point is another. We feel that this heritage and these values are in difficulty today, they suffer from insidious threats that therefore need a new sap to give new and further fruits. They are not lost values and goods, as someone believes or wants to make people believe. If that were the case, it could not be us here and today to make this talk of renewal and to draw the necessary reforms; others, rightly, would be in our place representatives of political and social forces of other nature and character. There would be a break in our constitutional history and a new constituent power, and not already a constituted power as we are and must not forget to be. In order to give new life to our Constitution, it is necessary to put hand to its second part, to the institutional mechanisms of representation and decision-making. This is the fundamental indication of the resolutions that were approved by the House and the Senate that established the bicameral commission twenty years ago. I know that some people object that the Constitution cannot be split in two like an apple and say that half is good and one is bad. But it is true - and we cannot forget this - that the institutions, their character, their rules of operation have autonomy, a reason to be peculiar and autonomous. There is - we would be wrong to deny it and always explain everything with politics - a technique of institutions; it is taught by all the constitutionalism that has developed in this century and that, not by chance, has received its most significant definition in that of "technique of freedom." I believe, therefore, not only in the possibility, but in the urgency of institutional reforms, also for the personal affair of this last decade, for the responsibilities that have been entrusted to me and that have allowed me a general view of the problems and the functioning of our State. There are, of course, deep knots to dissolve, new structures to outline. This indication also comes from the movement that has developed in the country, from a request of the citizens who already strongly expressed themselves with the referendum on the single preference in June 1991 and above all, I want to stress, from the political vote that gave rise to the current legislature. The structure of Parliament, the relationship between the central and autonomous states, the mechanisms of representation and the decision of government, the structures for the implementation and guarantee of individual and collective rights - we must acknowledge this - are no longer adequate and do not facilitate further progress in their present form. Probably in the face of these fundamental problems there were inadequate and old solutions and mechanisms at the very time when the Constitution was deliberate. But even if this had not been so, history has also walked much and tumultuously in these decades. Challenges and new problems have been imposed inside and outside each country; profound, sometimes shocking, transformations have come about. They range from the modernity and rapidity of communication between individuals, first unthinkable (so much that we speak today of planet-village) to the discoveries and applications of science that touch, with the themes of bioethics, the most intimate fibers of man. Faced with all this, in order for the fundamental values of our Constitution to continue and develop again, we need updated mechanisms of overall government of the country, of a government of our society that is not only democratic, but even more democratic, if compared with the past. In this work that we are going to do there are in us - and I am talking above all about those who are closest to my generation - complex feelings, perhaps contradictory, concerns, sometimes doubts. There is especially bitterness because the problems could have been solved in the past, they should not have reached such a level of complexity and degradation. But there is also the conviction that there is still time to act and there is also - allow me this term - the pride that, despite everything, for the strength of our people, for the battles that in the squares and in the places of work are led to defend social and civil conquests, remains open for our country a perspective of democracy, of desire and demand for participation and, despite everything, of hope and trust in the future. Italy, ladies and gentlemen, is not a country on its knees (as you want us to believe, I stress) and I realize the seriousness of this statement made at such a time. In his people, in his people there are still great moral resources, great energies that it would be our job to deploy. But be careful! Here there is all the great responsibility of a ruling class that must not and can no longer afford the luxury of error and delay. We must move forward with clear and well-thought-out choices, with responsible proposals, that touch all open themes and know how to see them together in any case, even if then everyone will have their own specific solution. We must have the ambition to outline structures capable of governing, certainly not for eternity but at least for a reasonable temporal perspective, the life of our country. This is not the time to enter into the merit of the problems, but it is beyond doubt that the most delicate point, where the most contrasts, uncertainties and even the change of opinions, is precisely what intertwines the mechanism of representation with the formation of the Government. I believe that this issue today is made even more complex, because to it the crisis of political parties has been added and superimposed, which concerns not only their ability to live coalitions and programmes, but it is within their intimate structure: it concerns their link with social demands, their ability to interpret them and place them in an ideal and general project perspective; it concerns profound degenerations that are boundless in the abuse of public authorities, in the theft of public money. We must be aware of this and this must make us cautious in thinking that all the problems will be resolved by a single reform. Let us look at other European experiences to learn from the structures they have experienced, as well as from the reflections, doubts and reformist intentions that exist in those countries and that must not be considered little. If we could move with this awareness, but at the same time with the desire to do and not find alibi to postpone and sink, we would be able to find the way and reach the goal. Of course, as I said before, the proposal should have been clear, understandable to the citizens. All institutional mechanisms, and in particular electoral ones, do not exist in the pure, ideal state, but are always empirical elaborations. Their basic approach must be united and coherent in order to have strength, to enter the political civilization of a people and then, over time, to constitute a tradition. This has happened with the proportional principle in Italy in these Republican decades. Many have now recognized its important merits in the development of democracy, but today limits and shortcomings are felt. The new system, to which it was necessary to go - and I stress above all the results of the referendum of 9 June 1991 - should, even more so than the old one, have made everyone feel that they counted, that they were represented, that they had a voice of proposal in Parliament, but also a voice of protest. The new system should certainly not feed fragmentation or dispersion, but it should not disperse differences and originality, nor political traditions. Behind a political force are the real citizens, with their social place, their history and their faith, with their hopes and expectations. They are not - we are not - all equal, all transformable, transferable or homologable. Of course, the political forces we know are not a fact that must be valid for eternity, but we cannot even think that they can disappear overnight. If this were to happen, it would probably be a colossal chameleon operation, a pure transformation, on the basis of which we would find ourselves faced with the oldest in the previous parties, with a new label. The road is another: that of the renewal of parties, radical and profound, made with an open face, in a transparent way and under the control of the public opinion, that must see change not so much and not only the faces, but also the methods of action, behavior in public responsibilities, the choices and selections of the new ruling groups, which must be democratic and crystalline, so that we can not say that the wolf loses his hair but not the vice. I wanted to stress this point because our work of institutional reform, in order to succeed, should be accompanied by the reform of the political system and the regeneration of political parties. Ladies and gentlemen, Parliament must not fail in its task: it has a great responsibility which we must recognise and which must lead us to act, each for its part, with determination, but with a clearness of intent and proposals. To lower the quality of the comparison here, the rigour in advancing their arguments and the ability to be flexible in the elaboration of possible synthesis would be a serious damage (it would be better to change, these days it is better to explain it) for all and would weaken Parliament's role and prestige in the reform process. I very much hope that there will be everyone's attention in carrying out the joint work and, with regard to Parliament's responsibility, I would like to stress the importance of not having touched on the establishment of Article 138 of the Constitution. I want to stress the importance of not having touched on the establishment of Article 138 of the Constitution, and I want to stress the importance of not touching it, as the Presidents of the Chambers had rightly advised in the document they drafted and proposed to the parliamentary groups (yes, it happened: inform you). 18.59 p.m. (EPE follow-up). This, not only because there is a framework of guarantees in it that should not have been mitigated, or at least altered, but because, by modifying that article or derogating from them, we would have told the public and the country that this process of revision means in some way a break, even if limited, of the continuity of our democratic order as it emerged from the constituent Assembly. A difficult task, therefore, awaits us. Our responsibility - as I have said several times - is enormous. If we fail and if we miss this occasion that we ourselves, on the basis of a deep and widespread demand of the citizens, have sought and created, certainly not everything will be lost, but the future history of the country (and also ours) will be more difficult and more suffering. And here twenty years ago there were applause and many congratulations. By the way, those who have laid it down have also realized it, because Article 7 says that: The Committee shall cease its duties by the publication of the law or laws (...). Already this rule leads us to an interpretative effort, that is: does the Committee dissolve or not, when the first law is passed? Perhaps not, but we have written that it can be dissolved with the approval of the first law. Continuing the reading of the article, it refers to constitutional laws, but also to ordinary laws approved under this Constitutional Law. Therefore, it is assumed that the laws in question can never be one or in any case it is very likely that one is not limited to one. So we should wait for the approval - indeed, the publication - of constitutional and ordinary laws related to constitutional laws, which are the subject of this bill. So when does the Committee end? When does it turn off? The Committee shall cease in the event that I have just mentioned, i.e. when at least one of the Chambers is dissolved. We, therefore, have before us a legislature that will lead us to 2018, in which we will discuss many constitutional and ordinary laws; it may also be that some of them are approved. Surely all constitutional ones, in accordance with Article 5 of the draft law under consideration, must be - or rather, may - subject to referendums. Under the terms laid down in Article 5, they may be submitted to a referendum if one fifth of the members of a House, or five hundred thousand voters, or five regional councils so require: so there are also conditions for referendums to be promoted. I do not want to delay then on the various notes I took about when the Presidents of the Chambers intervene to establish the terms, about when they take the first three months and the second three months, about the next 45 days, about the ten days, which are attached to the next 45 days, about the six months and about what else we can identify as instrumentation relating to the terms set out in the provision. I found this very complex, even from the point of view of interpretation. The rules of this law are intertwined with the regulations. Unfortunately, that will not be the case. ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u156	President, Minister, Senator citizens, I agree with Senator Uras: there would actually be so many rules that should be repealed or, as they say, simplified. For example, during the 14th legislature, with the signature of the Minister of Justice Castelli and the President of the Republic Carlo Azeglio Ciampi, under Law No 85 of 2006, over 14 articles of the penal code have been amended and in some cases decriminalized and/or repealed. On this subject, we were told that it was necessary to eliminate, modify or decriminalise a series of crimes that had nothing to do with the freedom of thought and opinion that characterizes a democratic state. Not being, ladies and gentlemen, obviously a so-called judicialist, I could have rejoiced at the intervention of decriminalization of certain facts of crime that were indicated as crimes of opinion, but at a careful reading of articles subject to modification, repeal or decriminalization, we find some very delicate and important articles for which we cannot (but have done so) talk about crimes of opinion. These were attacks against the integrity and unity of the State, subversive or subversive associations, propaganda and subversive activities, but above all attacked against the Constitution of the State. These crimes, like to say, retouched or taroccati concern (in fact, they concerned) conducts much more alarming and above all referable also to those who, through their institutional position, attacked the Constitution by means not allowed by the constitutional order, that is from the inside of the State itself, such as the attack against the Constitution of the State, provided for by Article 283 of the penal code and subject, as we will see, to change by the Berlusconi Government in 2006. On the basis of the original text of Article 283 of the Criminal Code, Loggia Propaganda 2 (the so-called Loggia P2) was considered illegal, of which Senator Silvio Berlusconi himself was a member. On 25 January 2006, the parliamentary majority, led by Berlusconi, approved an amendment to Article 283 of the Criminal Code, reducing the minimum imprisonment from 12 to 5 years and including as constituent elements of the case punishable by acts of violence. The previous text was as follows: "Anyone who commits a fact intended to change the Constitution of the State or the form of the Government by means not permitted by the constitutional order of the State shall be punished with imprisonment not less than 12 years old" (Article 283 of the Criminal Code before the legislative novella of 2006). On the other hand, the text following the amendment is as follows: "Anyone who, by violent acts, commits a direct and suitable act to change the Constitution of the State or the form of Government, shall be punished with imprisonment not less than five years." (This is the new text.) This change was an expression of a very little reformist will, but very much to protect those who work within the organs of the State. It should be remembered that the original case of crime, aimed at protecting our Constitution, was introduced by the constituent Assembly itself by law of November 11, 1947, n. 1317. This integration was justified by the new constitutional structure of the State, as well as the need to fully protect the delicate mechanism referred to in Article 138, heart and core of our Constitution, protecting not only from violent activities, but also from activities carried out in violation of the constitutional order through the distorted use of prerogatives of those who have institutional roles. Very often the Constitution is used as a lever for purposes far from it. Too often it is invoked by distorting its value and meaning. You will not want me to, Mr President, but the Constitution came to the fore in this Chamber to block a parliamentary debate on an international treaty. President Grasso remembers that, but so do the other colleagues, I presume. I am referring to the Franco-Italian international treaty on the construction of a railway line. This ignorance of the dictates of our fundamental Charter is certainly not a good feature that qualifies you as suitable for amending the Constitution. And to delegate only some of you, of course, is not a better guarantee but a tragedy announced. I fear the time when you will go to modify this article, because it will be the grinder, the break of the seal. Well, our Constitution is a very delicate creature, already considered as an obstacle by the Loggia Propaganda 2 by Licio Gelli. It is regrettable that few people remember the episode of 2006 that I already mentioned: the Berlusconian majority had the article that was supposed to defend Article 138 of our Constitution modified. For the inattentive to my first part of the exhibition I refer to Article 283 of the penal code, on the basis of which the so-called Loggia P2 was considered illegal, of which Silvio Berlusconi himself was a member. But this rule could hinder possible attacks on the Constitution from within the palace. Some so-called constitutional barricades, to protect the Constitutional Charter itself, have already fallen as a result of the legislative blows inflicted by a subject that will soon have to get away from this House, by a subject that still arouses admiration and gratitude in PD (cited by Letta: "A great!'). These words refer only to Article 138 of the Constitution: The simplest criteria have also been adopted for the constitutional revision process, without recourse to the systems of approval in two successive legislatures or the automatic dissolution of the Chambers after they approved the revision at first reading. There will have to be two readings, and with a significant interval ("to think about it"), in the same legislature. If the Constitution is to be rigid, too cumbersome and complicated a revision procedure would hinder the path to a completion of the constitutional building; that we want both in its great definitive walls and have life for centuries but it may be necessary to put your hands on the developments, not yet exactly predictable, of constitutional systems. Of course, ladies and gentlemen, before the final approval of Article 138 of the Constitution, there was even talk of revision of the Constitution and adoption of constitutional laws through a mechanism that even provided for the intervention of two legislatures or the dissolution of the Chambers between first and second reading. But have you read the text presented to you? Have you understood what this Committee is? A mini-constituent assembly that melts like snow in the sun at every legal approval? A new Parliament? The embryo of the new and future constitutional structure, as intended by the Loggia Propaganda 2, 3 or 4? How will it affect the quorums of the Chambers' sessions? I would like to make you think about this. On the enormous discretion of the President of the Committee, who, by setting the dates of the meetings, may also affect the work of the Chambers by reducing, as he pleases, the legal number of meetings of the Chambers themselves. This is set out in Article 1 (7) of the text under observation, which reads: At the meetings of their respective Assemblies, the members of the Committee absent, as they are engaged in the work of the Committee, are not counted for the purposes of the quorum. Believe me, in fact, believe the Constitutional Court: the deadly attacks on the Basic Charter of the State are taking place slowly. The constituent assembly, even before the definitive entry into force of our Constitution, wanted in advance to defend it, precisely, with the law November 11, 1947, n. 1317, which introduced the crime of attack on the Constitution, because it knew that the attack could be non-violent, but carried out through means not permitted by the constitutional order of the State, as stated in article 283 of the penal code before the intervention of the defenders of the Loggia P2. Ladies and gentlemen, the attacks on the Constitution are taking place slowly and the constituent Assembly had understood it, it had anticipated it. The same cumbersome quota of the affairs manageable by the Committee appears to be a slap in the face of the so-called "think about" indicated in the report of the President of the Constitution. If it were not for the intervention of the Loggia P2 on Article 283 of the Criminal Code it would be found in the conduct of those who have drafted the text now in question the case of attack on the Constitution through methods not allowed. In conclusion, I repeat that in the constitution there was even talk of revision of the Constitution and adoption of constitutional laws through a mechanism that provided for the intervention of two legislatures or the dissolution of the Chambers before the second reading. Do you understand, dear colleagues, what did the constituent Fathers who wrote this part of the Constitution mean? Were they too protective? I don't think so! Were they afraid of ad hoc changes? More likely! Probably, we could also discuss the issue raised by my colleague and understand how presidentialism can be combined with the exercise of direct democracy, if we were in a truly democratic country where the life of the community is based on many shared values and few written rules. But, alas, rules in the desert of values proliferate in our country. In this scenario, which perhaps escapes the short-sighted eye of my colleague, it is clear that presidentialism would be a source of misfortune that Italians - we are sure - want to do without. On the Constitution we believe that the only person protected to judge and amend can only be the people, that is, the whole of all citizens who, because of their dignity and an unbearable power of criticism, can express themselves on the quality of the constitutional dictation. Mr Zanettin's remarks were followed by those of Senator Bisinella, to whom, evidently, the night did not bring advice, if this morning she threw herself without parachute on the ground of cat-pardism, accusing us of being its new bearers, the renovators. Said by the representative of a political force that, in the crucial phases in which our country could really renew itself, has been complicit in preserving the status quo and, indeed, in strengthening the Oligarchic party model that gave us an army of nominations, is at least bizarre. Finally, Mr Carraro, when he complains that the rules governing the functioning of Parliament are obsolete, gives me the opportunity to make the following observation instead. The problem is not the obsolescence of Parliament's rules. The problem is that the above rules are very often poorly applied, and I would like to give an example: the work done in the Commissions, which could actually function as excellent working groups, because in those places we often manage to strip away ideologies - those which are obsolete - and to work on the substance and content, is often underused or badly used, because of the perennial emergency in which we find ourselves having to work. Having said that, I will turn to a comment on the information of Minister Quagliariello, having seen in it numerous paradoxes. The first paradox is that of haste. We have read that, in this process, Parliament remains absolutely sovereign in its ability and prerogatives. But then it is said that the goal is to bring home the reform quickly, fast times that do not fit with the parliamentary dialectic. We have been told that the Commission of Wise Men is composed of lawyers and scholars with different cultural and political-institutional sensibilities, but among all these sensibilities it does not seem to be understood the sensitivity to the constitutional dictation, to the point that all the reform process we are discussing is based on irrital procedures invented ad hoc and by derogation - a word that today is very fashionable - from the system of weights and counterweights established by the constituent Fathers, in order to avoid that a group of people more or less wise could put their hands in haste and fury to the Charter founded on our civil coexistence, without the absolute guarantee that the changes would take place in compliance with the rules and minorities. I am referring not only to the minorities present in this House, but also to all those social and cultural minorities which are not represented in this Parliament, and the fact of abstentionism proves their existence. These social and cultural minorities could have expressed themselves more easily, if the debate on such sensitive issues had involved the entire public opinion, and not only a small group of essays, whose wisdom cannot, however, be a legitimization to the self-referentiality of the political class, to the hastyness or to the use of procedures ad reformam, invented of sound plant in order to make a process that, in the intentions of the constituent Fathers, had instead to be accurate and would have needed time. The Minister made us aware that the Commission would deepen the reasons underlying the fragility of our political and institutional system, without grasping the paradox that the Commission is not a solution to this fragility, but is the most mature and tangible result of it. In other words, the paradox is that we are trying to solve the evils of politics by creating a bigger one and establishing a precedent that in the future will allow anyone, with the most varied intentions, to invoke crises, states of institutional emergency, natural disasters, invasions of grasshoppers and alarms of various kinds as shortcuts to cut and paste pieces of Constitution with the same ease that could be used to put hand, perhaps, to a regulation of condominium. But the comparison is probably improper, because with regard to the condominium regulations the administrators diligently observe the rigid procedures of modification provided for by the civil code. No leader-condom, in fact, would dream of having a new regulation approved by a group of wise men chosen between friends and acquaintances, although potentially esteemable in their wisdom. Mr Quagliariello then opens another remarkable paradox when he talks about bipolarism in a tripolar Parliament. I understand the process of removal and the attempt to escape from a painful reality: one escapes in the illusion of still living in bipolarism, but - alas - Minister we are also in this Parliament and also with important numbers. Among these instruments we find a confidence that even monocamerals would want, as if the bicameral one had not already defiled the function of Parliament, with the continuous use and abuse of the vote of confidence by the last governments, including the one currently in office. The Minister - you - has also informed us that there is still an urgent need to correct the current electoral law, and it is paradoxical to hear this statement from a politician who has been sitting in Parliament since 2006 and who in 2013 is part of a political formation and a government that have continued to bypass the crucial hub of the electoral law using it to the need as a pretext for emergency to form technical governments or broad understandings, but in fact ignoring this issue to focus on this great work of constitutional engineering that is fascinating, yes, many wise men, but is also worrying several citizens. However, I would like to express the conviction that, from 2005 until now, the authority and stability of the institutions have not been put at risk by a Constitution that someone has an interest in describing as obsolete, but by the loss of credibility of the institutions themselves for the bleak spectacle they have provided us, by an electoral law renamed "porcellum" and by the absence of a vote of preference, which is another of our paradoxes. It is paradoxical, in fact, that the restoration of the vote of preference for the political elections is not recognized as a fundamental step to restore to politics dignity, representation and legitimacy, but only as one of the many options taken into consideration by the so-called sages.
