ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u165	Mr President, the importance of the Constitution has long been known: Herodotus said that the Constitution guarantees the wealth and future of a society. I think that what today could be misinterpreted as obstructionism, but that we like to identify as constructionism, is the highest respect of the Constitution, understood not only as respect for the constituent Fathers and our country, but precisely as the right of citizens to be self-determined, to choose their own Parliament, their own Government and also, as parliamentarians, to exercise the full right to legislate. Today, practically everything has been said, even with regard to the concerns that an amendment of the Constitution can create and, therefore, to those doubts that arise every time that legitimacy and legality, meaning by legitimacy the adequacy to concrete expectations and, by legality, compliance with abstract rules, do not coincide. When these do not coincide, the norm can be respected, but the content fails. I sincerely believe that changes are necessary and time-consuming, but I do not think that the 5 Star Movement is rigid and closed to a change in the Constitution, what gives rise to concern is the way, the principle and, above all, the fact that it is not clear where the amendment goes. Is it obscure and how can a citizen share a change that is not able to learn, verify and see? Calamandrei - today he has been quoted several times - spoke of the Constitution as "great unfinished." I sincerely see the physical, environmental, cultural, social and political landscape of our country and I know that in many places this beautiful Constitution is unfinished. I would like to emphasise an article that is fundamental to me, Article 9, which solemnly enshrined the indissoluble union of historical, artistic and landscape heritage and has transformed into a project an ethical and political role that this union has played in the history of Italy. The Constitutional Court has repeatedly expanded this very original vision by becoming aware that the first and most essential common good is the environment, whose protection, in the name of the public interest, is an essential condition for the very existence of a modern democracy. I would like to point out that that great project, the Constitution, born of an Italy in tears and rags, we have so far not been able to translate it into practice; we have not yet understood it and translated it into practice, but we are about to modify it. Article 9 is important because it promotes culture, scientific research and technology, protects the landscape, the historical and artistic heritage of the nation. This article is essential to the legality of a Republic, one and indivisible (Article 5) and relates to other articles of the Constitution. It should, for example, be understood as an expression of those indispensable duties of political, economic and social solidarity of Article 2 and addressed, like others, to the full development of the human personality (Article 3), linked to freedom of thought and speech (Article 21), freedom of art, science and teaching (Article 33), to the centrality of the state public school (Article 34). The protection of the landscape contributes to the formation of the concept of the environment, as a primary constitutional value, converging with the protection of health as the fundamental right of the individual and the interest of the community (Article 32). According to the Constitution, the common good does not compress, but limits the rights of individuals and businesses, i.e. private property. The social function must be ensured (Article 42), and freedom of enterprise cannot be carried out in conflict with social usefulness, in such a way as to harm security, freedom and human dignity (Article 41). Is it not that perhaps we want to change Article 41 for the worse - I would be rather concerned about that - so that freedom of enterprise may run counter to social usefulness or harm security, freedom and human dignity? Is that what we want to write in our Constitution? Our Constitution was and is in tune with the great cultural trends of our time. "Ethics and aesthetics," as Wittgenstein said, "are one thing" and "ethics" must be a condition of the world, such as logic." We must react against indifference, which kills democracy, we must react against the real anti-politics, which is certainly not ours. The anti-politics is that of the markets, to which every day the right and the left are genuflected: it is the markets that limit the guarantee of public freedom and civil rights. We want to recover the Community spirit, thinking of freedom, equality and the right to work: this is also an unfulfilled right, today more than ever. Culture cannot be separated from the complex production activities of a people. In order to conduct this battle, the best weapon is the Constitution, the community of citizens source of the laws and holder of the rights, the norm that makes the law of ethics and universal inspiring principles. That is why the Constitution must be safeguarded, that is why we must reflect, that is why every mistake and every hasty change must be avoided. We must gain sovereignty in the name of the Constitution, we must legitimise this representative democracy and then, if possible, aspire to a direct democracy. It is true: there are many shadow cones in this unfinished Constitution. I am thinking, for example, of the landscape, of the cases in which the protection of the landscape itself squeals with urban rules and, with which cities and the quality of our daily life are not protected. The Constitution can also be changed, but according to the rules, considering that the various articles must not constitute a litany of principles detached from each other, but must follow its spirit, as has often been pointed out by several judgments. The Constitutional Court has worked a lot in this direction: think, for example, of the judgment in which it is stated, precisely, that the protection of the landscape must not be subordinate to other values, such as those of an economic nature, but must be able to influence deeply the political and social order. Our Constitution has not only remained unfinished, but has also often been betrayed and dismantled. So we ask for an act of responsibility and reflection, to consciously change it all together. It is true: the Constitution is a great unfinished one, but perhaps, in a certain sense, it is good that it is so, because the Constitution must be implemented every day. It is a 1975 message, which the then president Giovanni Leone sent to the Chambers. That's what he said: Our Constitution outlines a regime of freedom, democracy and social justice that focuses on the principle of respect and valorization of the dignity of the human person, through the wider freedom of expression of thought, political organization, trade union association; the consecration of the principle of equality that the State must promote through the realization of fundamental social rights; and the definition of institutional arrangements that, by being a pivot on Parliament, aim to guarantee the citizen, in a widespread and rich pluralism, the effective participation in the life of the State. If the Constitution is this harmonious set of norms and institutions, the democratic regime that follows must be the exemplary testimony. Yet a situation of discomfort dominates the country and characterizes the crisis, whose solution must be sought precisely in the principles and institutions of the Constitutional Charter and in their full implementation. A system like ours, which for about thirty years has been able to withstand the impact of exceptional events and threats to the process of economic and moral elevation of the country and which has been able to create and guarantee a legislative and civil fabric of very high democratic content, does not require a substantial reworking but requires, as far as institutions and norms are concerned, its more organic and balanced implementation in all constitutional, political and social articulations and potentialities. More recent (18 July 2006) is the bill signed by Mancino, Villone, Manzella, Maccanico, Zavoli, Sinisi and Binetti in whose report reads: Ladies and gentlemen, the transition in 1993 from the system proportional to that of the majority for the election of the two Chambers, in addition to producing, together with other causes, a strong push to bipolarize the political groups, has also provoked a crisis of the rigidity of our Constitution. As is well known, the rigid nature of the Constitutional Charter represents, together with the independence of the guarantee bodies (President of the Republic and Constitutional Court), the most robust garrison to prevent the Constitution from becoming an instrument of the policy of the winning coalition in the general elections. Enzo Cheli recently stated that "the Constitution belongs to (...) all citizens and must therefore be removed from the availability of the majority, indeed it must operate essentially as a system of limits to the majority" (the Constitutional State, Roots and Perspectives, Naples, Scientific Editorial, 2006, page 15). In order to achieve this objective, the procedure for revising the Constitutional Charter (Article 138 of the Constitution) must be seriously different from that adopted for ordinary legislation. The procedural worsening provided for in the provision now cited has proved inadequate since 1994 to guarantee the superiority of the Constitution over ordinary law, since the new electoral laws allowed to reach the absolute majority of the members of the two Chambers even to coalitions that represented only the strongest minority in the electoral corps. This situation has not changed with the last electoral law, which still provides for a majority award under a system based on the proportional principle. Moreover, comparative law includes experiences in which the characteristics of the proportional system adopted favour the achievement of majority results; just give the example of Spain. In the end, Article 138, with the current discipline, tempts majorities to act alone in the modifications of the Constitution that the Constituent had entrusted to a resolution based on a broader consensus, in the context of a truly proportional system, in the rules and in the results, such as that accepted on the agenda Giolitti. In fact, to this temptation they have yielded both the center-left majority, with the reform of Title V (Constitutional Law 18 October 2001, n. 3) in the XIII legislature, and that of center-right in the XIV, with the parliamentary approval of a very large text amending the II Part of the Constitution, then not confirmed by the electoral corps in the referendum of 25-26 June 2006. (...) This bill has a similar precedent in the Constitutional Law No 2115 presented to the House in the 12th legislature at the initiative of Mr Bassanini and Mr Elia, to which were added the signatures of very authoritative parliamentarians, such as Mr Napolitano, Mr Mattarella, Mr Veltroni and Mr Fassino. That proposal could not be examined for the early dissolution of the Chambers decreed by the President of the Republic at the beginning of 1996. It should also be remembered that the content of the constitutional bill that we submit to your examination has been considered of priority importance in the programme submitted, according to electoral law, to the electoral judgment of the parties gathered in the center-left coalition led by Romano Prodi. It is therefore necessary, at the beginning of the 15th Republican legislature, to start the process of a reform of high guarantee significance, in the context of constitutionalism accepted in the Constitutional Charter. I shall conclude and thank you for your willingness to listen to your last speech. There is a lot of truth - I say this by addressing, in particular, my colleagues in the 5 Star Movement to whom I also say that I did not understand, beyond the more or less well-founded criticisms, what they would like in terms of institutional arrangements - in the resignation and criticism that emerged in this debate, all the more so when we look at the past. However, our task now is to look ahead and it is on these issues and on the courageous ability to self-form, even by deleting positions and privilege procedures, that we will be evaluated above all by the Italians. ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u171	I'm asking to talk.
ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u172	You have the option, exceptionally, for three minutes.
ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u173	D'ALI' (PdL). Mr President, I am paying for a short departure from the House with a reduction in time. I would simply like to reiterate our conviction that this path we have begun, having had the experience in past legislation of many attempts that have not been successful and being myself, moreover, convinced of the desirability of a complete modification of Part II of the Constitution (I have heard fears of possible interventions on the part I who are not, moreover, provided for by this constitutional law). I would like to thank the Minister for the work he has done and for the elegance with which he brought to Parliament the conclusions of the Commission he chaired, with a very small regret for what I am concerned with. My position is well known with regard to a need which, in my opinion, arises for a stronger reform of Title V of the Constitution: not only a new discipline of competences, of competing legislation, of the prevailing legislation, but also with courage to tackle precisely the organization of the institutions on the territory, of the local autonomy, the reduction of the number of centres of expenditure. In fact, the large public expenditure that currently afflicts the country and under which the country risks being crushed in large part is due to the excessive number of points of expenditure: 8,200 municipalities, of which the vast majority with less than 3,000 inhabitants; 110 Provinces (and intervention on the Provinces is not enough to raise public spending); a huge myriad of intermediate bodies. The same 20 Regions, in my opinion, are absolutely exorbitant compared to what would be the needs of this country; we have Regions that, by size, are inferior to a neighborhood of the big cities. Above all, we need a new design of competences through levels of government that can be assembled. For example, metropolitan areas, in my opinion, should be areas with exclusive legislative autonomy and with competences including those that today have municipalities, provinces and regions themselves. The citizen is entitled to a simplification of the contact points for his/her needs and points for the provision of services. If we do not start rationalization, if we do not refuse to suffer the pressures of the local policy that wants the parcelling of the agencies for the proliferation of places of possible employment, I do not think we will have worked to offer the country that service of total constitutional reorganization that it asks us. With regard to your determinations, I thank the Minister again and you, Mr President, for the time you have given me. I hope to be able to address these issues in the light of the debate that the Committee will have to address. [[Applause from Senator Liuzzi.]]
ParlaMint-IT_2013-10-16-LEG17-Senato-sed-126.ana.u174	The general debate is closed. After the wide-ranging debate today, I wish all my colleagues and especially the Minister, given his state of health, a healthy and robust constitution.
